7 Tool Calling

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a tool call that parses cleanly still need a runtime between it and the world?

A tool call is the first point at which a model’s output can change something outside the conversation. A proposal that parses cleanly can still name a target the task was never granted, act on a file that has changed since the model read it, or repeat an operation whose first attempt already took effect. Generating the call therefore cannot be what authorizes it. The runtime must stand between proposal and effect, validating each call against its schema, checking it against the grants the task holds, dispatching it, and recording what is actually known about the result. That record is often incomplete. A request that times out may have been lost, may still be running, or may have succeeded without a delivered reply, and a blind retry can restart healthy workers or apply the same change twice. The outcome has to stay explicitly unknown until the runtime settles it, rather than be filled in by the model’s account or the runtime’s optimism. What returns to the model is itself a design decision. A log far longer than the next call can hold must be cut down without hiding the line that matters, and a job that outlives one turn must report back without being mistaken for a finished one. Tool calling keeps the model a proposer and leaves authority with the runtime, which exercises it only on evidence it can check.

Learning Objectives
  • Trace a proposed tool call through validation, authorization, dispatch, and a correlated observation into the next model call
  • Design a tool descriptor that separates structural validation from action-time preconditions enforced with the effect
  • Distinguish tool discovery and connectivity from the grants required to invoke an operation
  • Select blind retry, endpoint deduplication, or reconciliation for an uncertain effect from operation semantics and loss rates
  • Design bounded observations that preserve execution status, omission information, and the scope of available evidence
  • Compare waiting and resumption strategies for long-running tools by detection delay, inference cost, and retained supervision
  • Evaluate toolkit granularity by context cost, cost per accepted task, and enforced reach across structured, general-purpose, and computer-use interfaces
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

7.1 The Tool Boundary

An agent asked to restart a container on a staging server, the kind of infrastructure change the remediation agent (Four lighthouse workloads) makes, emits a short tool call. The host runtime checks the call against the tool’s schema, confirms that this session may restart that container, and sends POST /containers/srv-stage-04/restart. Two seconds later the socket times out. The request may be lost, delayed, still executing, or completed without a delivered reply. A retry could restart healthy workers, and the first attempt can still take effect after the timeout.

The Authority axis, closed at the tool boundary by mediating every proposed call.

Nothing about this failure is exotic. The repair agent meets the same questions on every turn of the connection-pool repair. It reads src/pool.py, replaces text derived from the version it read, runs the test suite, and receives a log far longer than the next model call can hold. Each step is a tool call, and each can be rejected, left running, completed without a reply, or answered with more output than the model should see.

The pieces built so far do not settle these questions. Messages and tool definitions gave the model a structured way to propose a call. The memory chapters already relied on tools to read sources, search indexes, and update records without examining what happens between a proposal and its result. A retrieval result identifies the version it observed, but whether the file still has that version when a later edit commits is an action-time precondition that something must enforce. A proposal names an operation and its arguments. It does not decide whether this task may run the operation, whether an earlier attempt already ran it, or what the next model call should receive.

The runtime’s tool boundary is the path between a proposed call and the environment it would change. The boundary validates each proposal, checks it against the task’s grants, dispatches it, tracks what is known about its effect, and returns a bounded observation matched to the call. Throughout, it keeps apart the three things the timeout blurs: what the model proposed, what effect the operation had, and what evidence the runtime holds about either. The chapter follows one call from proposal to observation, then widens to effects whose outcome stays uncertain, to output too large to return whole, to jobs that outlive a model turn, and finally to the catalog of tools the model sees.

↩︎ The separation of proposal, effect, and observation is established in Why the runtime manages the trajectory, not the single call.

7.2 Tool Subsystem Architecture

When the socket times out on restart_container, a runtime that drops the failed call from history or reports it as a clean failure invites the next model turn to issue a duplicate restart. Listing 1 shows the staging restart as one turn of the loop, in a provider-neutral form. The runtime reports what it knows about this attempt, and the next model call receives that result together with the earlier request and proposal.

The runtime correlates each result with its corresponding call using an explicit identifier rather than message order alone. That identifier establishes message lineage across asynchronous turns, but correlation alone does not resolve whether the target service completed the requested mutation. Preventing duplicate restarts requires an operational contract for idempotency, developed in section 7.5. Even if the subsequent model turn assumes that the service recovered, the runtime preserves the underlying uncertainty. It authorizes a retry only when idempotent semantics, an endpoint deduplication contract, or reconciliation evidence that also rules out a still-delayed attempt supports it. Fresh evidence that the container looks unchanged is not enough on its own (section 7.5.1).

7.2.1 One mediated round trip

Between the tool_call and tool_result messages of listing 1 lies the runtime’s mediation path. If the runtime piped a model’s proposed call straight to an execution backend and piped the raw output straight back into the prompt, an unauthorized call would mutate the host and an unbounded log would overflow the next context window. Instead, once the inference engine emits a structured invocation block at the model-side call surface, execution passes entirely to the runtime, which mediates the round trip between the proposed call and the next turn’s observation across the four coordinated stages of figure 1: argument validation, policy authorization, dispatch with outcome tracking, and observation shaping.

↩︎ The six-phase turn lifecycle, whose validation, execution, and observation phases these four stages refine, is established in The six phases of a turn.

Request validation parses proposed arguments against the tool’s declared schema, verifying required fields, data types, value ranges, and relational invariants among parameters. Dynamic constraints that depend on volatile environment state cannot be settled at this boundary. The system must evaluate them at the execution boundary rather than inferring them from historical context. Next, operation authorization verifies the active session’s security grants and access policies against the targeted resource, for read operations as well as state mutations, and admits the call against its concurrency budgets and rate limits.

Following authorization, dispatch submits the permitted operation to the underlying execution provider while outcome tracking maintains execution identity and status. This tracking distinguishes among pre-dispatch rejections, active background tasks, successfully reported results, and unconfirmed execution states. A transport timeout indicates only that the runtime exceeded its waiting deadline, leaving the remote execution state unconfirmed. Finally, observation shaping maps the raw return value back to the originating call identifier, truncating payloads to fit context budgets, sanitizing terminal escape sequences, and tagging the output with accurate status metadata. If log output must be truncated, the payload explicitly discloses the omission; if a system returns an asynchronous job ticket, observation shaping reports it as an acknowledgment rather than a completed effect.

Certain validation checks must execute at the remote endpoint because safety requires atomicity with the state change itself. For example, an edit conditioned on a source version requires the version check to occur in lockstep with the update. Consequently, the host runtime and the remote endpoint each enforce their respective boundaries. The endpoint checks access independently, even for requests that the host runtime already cleared. This division is the end-to-end argument applied to system protection. An authorization check that depends on private endpoint state can be finalized only at that endpoint, which makes preliminary host-level checks useful optimizations rather than authoritative substitutes (Saltzer et al. 1984).

When the serving interface returns native structured call blocks, the runtime extracts the proposed operation and arguments directly. When a model emits proposals within unstructured free text, the runtime must first detect and parse those fragments before validation can proceed. Constrained decoding eliminates syntactic parsing failures by enforcing grammar rules directly during token generation. However, syntax alone never establishes operational safety. Whether the target resource exists, whether the observed version remains current, and whether the current session holds execution privileges remain runtime checks that no decoding grammar can verify.

7.2.2 Complete mediation

Just as a decoding grammar cannot verify execution privileges, a system-prompt instruction to touch only staging containers or read only inside /workspace places no barrier between a hallucinated or prompt-injected tool call and the host operating system. Securing the tool interface rests on complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975), the protection principle that already required the tool handler of Why token likelihood cannot replace checks to check a validated path again when the read executes. At the tool boundary, mediation must govern both reads and mutations. Emitting a tool name does not grant the model ambient runtime authority, nor does receiving an observation make external data trusted.

Definition 7.1: Complete mediation

Complete mediation requires every proposed access to pass through an enforced authorization boundary before it reaches the protected resource. In an agent runtime, the return path is also mediated so that observations enter context under explicit access, payload, and status contracts.

  1. Significance: A valid-looking call cannot bypass the checks governing what the session may read, change, or disclose.
  2. Distinction: Schema validation establishes structural admissibility, not permission. Observation normalization establishes a usable representation, not the truth of its contents or immunity to prompt injection, attacker-controlled content that attempts to redirect the model.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a prompt instruction such as “never delete production data” as an access barrier. The runtime must enforce the restriction even when the model proposes the prohibited action.

Stating complete mediation as a policy does not by itself guarantee that every tool route honors it. Anderson formalized the architectural mechanism required to make that guarantee hold across every access path: the reference monitor (Anderson 1972).

Definition 7.2: Reference monitor

Reference monitor is the enforcement component through which every access to a protected resource passes. It must be tamper-proof, always invoked, and small enough to verify. In an agent runtime, the host’s validation and authorization path is the reference monitor for proposed tool calls, and each endpoint’s access check is a second monitor for the resources that endpoint owns.

  1. Significance: The three requirements turn complete mediation from an intention into a checkable property of the runtime’s structure.
  2. Distinction: The policy specifies permitted accesses, whereas the reference monitor provides the non-bypassable mechanism that evaluates and enforces those rules on every attempt.
  3. Common pitfall: Adding a tool route that does not pass through the monitor, such as an unrestricted shell with network access beside a checked file adapter, which makes the adapter’s checks advisory.

These three requirements dictate concrete architectural choices at the tool boundary. Tamper-proofing ensures that neither generated model tokens nor untrusted data ingested from external systems can alter the authorization rules enforced by the runtime. Always-invoked mandates that every operational path pass through the monitor without exception. Exposing an unrestricted shell runner alongside a sandboxed file tool, for example, undermines the protection boundary, because the agent can bypass file restrictions by issuing shell commands. Finally, verifiability requires policy decisions to be deterministic, auditable software checks. Although an auxiliary classifier or guardrail model can score operational risk, probabilistic inference cannot be verified in the strict systems sense; the monitor itself must enforce deterministic access boundaries.

Two operational rules follow directly from complete mediation. The grant rule establishes that execution authority derives solely from the session’s active security grants, re-evaluated on every invocation. Read operations demand the same rigorous mediation as state mutations, because returning private records discloses sensitive data even when environment state remains unchanged. Nothing the model sees or emits enlarges those grants, whether a schema-valid call or a tool description claiming broad permission; later sections apply the rule to each new artifact they introduce. The evidence-scope rule establishes that an observation confirms only the specific metric or condition measured by the tool during that invocation. Reporting that an operating system process exited with code zero confirms only process termination; it does not prove overall task completion, which remains a distinct judgment governed by invariant closure (principle 1). Raw output text also remains untrusted data even after the runtime strips terminal formatting and normalizes metadata; observations supply empirical evidence of state, but they carry no ambient authority to dictate subsequent actions.

7.2.3 Batches and forced calls

When a single model response proposes reading src/pool.py and patching src/pool.py in the same batch, dispatching both calls in parallel yields non-deterministic results depending on which thread reaches the file first. When batched operations are strictly read-only and independent, such as reading three distinct source files, the runtime can dispatch them concurrently and correlate each returned observation with its respective call identifier. A failure in one read does not invalidate concurrent reads that succeeded. Every call in the batch must still pass validation and authorization individually and draw from a shared resource and rate-limit budget.

When tools declare a trustworthy resource footprint, the explicit read set \(\ToolCallReadSet_i\) and write set \(\ToolCallWriteSet_i\) of each call \(\ToolCallIndex\), the runtime can check whether batched mutations and reads interfere. Calls \(\ToolCallIndex\) and \(\PeerToolCallIndex\) may run concurrently when neither writes state the other reads or writes, \(\ToolCallWriteSet_i \cap (\ToolCallReadSet_j \cup \ToolCallWriteSet_j) = \emptyset\) and \(\ToolCallWriteSet_j \cap \ToolCallReadSet_i = \emptyset\), which are Bernstein’s conditions for noninterference (Bernstein 1966). Reads of two different files pass both conditions; a read and an edit of src/pool.py fail the first, because the edit writes the file the read reads. This guarantee, however, relies entirely on the completeness of footprint declarations; any hidden side effect or undeclared shared dependency invalidates the concurrency guarantee.

Bernstein, Arthur J. 1966. “Program Analysis for Parallel Processing.” IEEE Transactions on Electronic Computers EC-15 (5): 757–62. https://doi.org/10.1109/PGEC.1966.264565.

Operations with opaque side effects, such as arbitrary shell commands, have unknown footprints. In such cases, the runtime must either isolate the execution environment, serialize calls conservatively, or reject batched execution in favor of fine-grained tools. When operations have causal dependencies, those ordering constraints must come from the workflow policy or an explicit dependency graph. The sequential order of tokens generated by the model is not reliable evidence of intended execution order. Batching also does not imply atomicity; a batch forms a transaction only when the underlying tool substrate explicitly provides transactional all-or-nothing rollback semantics.

The runtime can also govern whether the model proposes an action. Using serving-level tool-choice control, the runtime can configure tool calling as optional, mandate that the model invoke at least one tool, restrict selection to a specific named operation, or forbid tool execution altogether. Orchestration engines use these constraints to guide execution phases, for instance forcing a diagnostic read before permitting an edit, or disallowing tool calls to force a final textual summary. Tool-choice controls, however, only shape model generation; under the grant rule, forcing a tool choice confers no operational authorization. Executing each stage of this mediated round trip requires an unambiguous request representation, establishing a strict operational boundary between static schema validation and dynamic preconditions verified at dispatch time.

7.3 Tool Interface Schemas

When the repair agent finishes inspecting revision r17 of src/pool.py and proposes a patch to produce r18, that boundary between static validation and dispatch-time checks becomes concrete. Before applying the modification to the workspace, the host runtime must resolve four questions: which tool operation the model selected, what its arguments denote, whether the current task possesses the authority required to execute the mutation, and whether src/pool.py still matches the revision r17 state on which the model based its proposal. A well-formed JSON payload satisfies syntactic structure, but syntax alone cannot answer these operational questions.

A tool descriptor defines an operation’s functional purpose, input parameters, expected return values, and execution side effects. Within that descriptor, the argument schema codifies machine-checkable constraints: required fields, primitive types, permitted enumerations, and supported size bounds. These specifications serve two distinct audiences: natural-language descriptions guide the language model in selecting and parameterizing tools, while the surrounding runtime and downstream service endpoints validate and enforce the interface contract.

7.3.1 Descriptor semantics

Even when every field passes JSON type checking, unstated edge cases in a descriptor can destroy workspace state: if a file-replacement tool leaves unspecified whether an empty replacement string is a no-op or a zero-byte write, a model attempting to leave src/pool.py unchanged will wipe the file. A tool such as conditional_replace_file, which replaces a single file in the task’s designated workspace and commits the change only if that file still matches the expected source version, must therefore state exact semantic invariants alongside its types. The path field resolves strictly relative to the designated workspace rather than an arbitrary host location, expected_version identifies the baseline source revision the model inspected, and replacement supplies the complete replacement content encoded in the endpoint’s documented format, with an empty string explicitly defined to truncate the file to zero bytes.

Full file replacement keeps this precondition example transparent, though production runtimes often prefer line-anchored patches or structured diffs to minimize token generation overhead. The interface comparison in Comparing Output Interfaces shows that localized edits reduce generated output while still demanding the same coordination between precondition verification and atomic mutation. Listing 2 illustrates the machine-checkable argument structure for that contract. The request payload contains neither a credential token nor a privilege flag through which the model might expand its access rights, and the host runtime independently binds the invocation to the task’s authorized execution context.

The descriptor’s result contract requires equal precision. A completed replacement reports the affected path and its newly assigned revision token. A version conflict explicitly reports that the target diverged and the replacement was not applied. The runtime must also distinguish deterministic client errors (malformed arguments or insufficient authority) from indeterminate transport failures where the endpoint’s state remains unknown after a dropped connection. A comprehensive descriptor models all four outcomes, including network timeouts where no acknowledgment arrives.

Precise field names and descriptions eliminate avoidable ambiguity during generation. For example, a file-reading tool should state whether its offset argument counts bytes, characters, or lines. Similarly, a timeout parameter must state its measurement units and specify whether it bounds connection establishment, remote execution, or end-to-end response delivery. While clear documentation guides the model toward valid parameters, the execution runtime must programmatically enforce all defaults, enumeration sets, and numerical bounds. Explicit tool documentation measurably improves the reliability of generation. Patil et al. (2023) demonstrated that models trained to condition tool invocations on retrieved API documentation invent nonexistent operations and hallucinate invalid arguments far less frequently than models relying solely on unconditioned prompting.

7.3.2 Structure, permission, and state checks

If generation halts at the output token limit or a client disconnection in the middle of a replacement string and a permissive parser closes the JSON object or accepts a partial payload, executing that call writes a half-finished src/pool.py to disk. Before parsing any arguments, the runtime therefore verifies that the model produced a completed candidate under the invocation contract defined in The Invocation Contract and aborts immediately on a truncated response. Once execution status confirms a complete output, the runtime parses the candidate and validates its arguments against the tool’s registered JSON schema before performing any domain checks.

In this replacement example, omitting expected_version or supplying a numeric path fails structural validation immediately. Setting "additionalProperties": false in listing 2 likewise causes the parser to reject hallucinated arguments such as force or recursive. However, structural conformance resolves only syntax. Verifying whether the path resides within the task’s assigned sandbox, and determining whether the target still contains the inspected source revision, require distinct evaluation steps. As summarized in table 1, the runtime enforces these guarantees across layered boundaries.

A string formatted as a file path does not constitute a confined resource reference.1 The tool implementation must canonicalize and resolve the target inside the authorized workspace, accounting for symlinks, directory traversal escapes, and path alias changes that could redirect the operation. Similarly, declaring replacement as a string does not bound its encoded memory footprint. Production adapters enforce explicit byte-size limits on incoming payloads, bound the volume of captured diagnostic output, and apply strict execution timeouts. Static schema validation handles input syntax, whereas dynamic state verification, path confinement, and resource throttling remain runtime obligations. Schema Rules for Tool Definitions supplies a bounded edit descriptor along with schema rules for required fields, size limits, explicit units, and variant semantics.

1 Time-of-Check to Time-of-Use: Bishop and Dilger analyzed the UNIX file-access race in which the binding of a name to an object changes between a program’s check and its later use, so a privileged program can be steered onto a file it never checked (Bishop and Dilger 1996). A tool adapter that validates src/pool.py and then reopens the path by name inherits the same window: a symbolic link swapped in between turns a permitted edit into an edit elsewhere. Resolving the path once and operating on the resolved object, rather than re-resolving the string, is what closes it.

Bishop, Matt, and Michael Dilger. 1996. “Checking for Race Conditions in File Accesses.” Computing Systems 9 (2): 131–52.

Grammar-guided decoding does not change this division of labor. A token-level mask removes syntax errors before the candidate exists, but it cannot resolve a filesystem reference, evaluate an access policy, or compare a version tag with the file’s current revision. The host runtime validates the materialized request, and the endpoint verifies dynamic preconditions at the moment the effect happens. This separation instantiates the typed action contract (principle 7). The contract admits a call to the environment only after validation, precondition, and authorization checks, with the version precondition enforced together with the mutation rather than during an earlier read.

↩︎ The structural guarantee of grammar-guided decoding, and the reference and permission checks it leaves open, are established in What a grammar mask guarantees.

7.3.3 Conditional updates

An earlier read confirmed only that src/pool.py matched revision r17 at the instant of observation. If another agent or background process modifies src/pool.py while the model is generating its patch, applying an unconditioned write silently overwrites those intervening changes. The expected_version parameter binds the retrieval contract of The Retrieval Contract directly to the mutation contract enforced here so that this lost-update race cannot succeed. To prevent the lost update, the storage service must verify the expected version and commit the replacement as a single atomic conditional update, the storage equivalent of a hardware compare-and-swap (CAS) primitive.2 In this example, the workspace source service executes the entire sequence inside a single coordination boundary: it resolves the target file, verifies that its current revision matches expected_version, writes the new content, and publishes the resulting revision token. Decomposing this sequence into non-atomic operations (reading the revision, verifying it in user space, then issuing a write) reopens a race window where concurrent writers can intervene. The atomic conditional update closes this time-of-check to time-of-use gap.

2 Optimistic Validation Under Contention: The expected-version check is commit-time validation from optimistic concurrency control, applied to one file. What is new at the tool boundary is the price of a conflict: each failed validation costs the agent a fresh read and a new proposal, paid in model turns rather than in microseconds.

↩︎ Why context engineering cannot close the refresh-to-execute race, and leaves the revision check to the tool boundary, is established in Detecting change and bounding uncertainty.

This execution branch yields two distinct operational outcomes:

  1. If the current source revision remains r17, the update commits the replacement and returns r18, identifying the new revision produced by the edit.
  2. If another writer modified the source first, the version check fails. The service rejects the replacement, leaves the storage state unaltered, and returns an explicit version-conflict status.

Following a conflict, the runtime directs the agent to perform an authorized read of the updated source file and synthesize a fresh proposal, rather than blindly overwriting the intervening modifications. This concurrency guarantee holds only as long as all mutations route through the shared coordination boundary. If external processes or unmanaged scripts modify files directly on disk, bypassing the version service, version tokens offer no protection and the endpoint cannot ensure integrity. Dry-run previews follow the identical principle. A dry run confirms that a proposed patch applies cleanly against revision r17 at the moment of evaluation, but it establishes no lock on that revision. The runtime must re-verify all preconditions, permissions, and version tokens when executing the live write.

Receiving a successful r18 confirmation verifies that the file modification committed to disk, but it does not establish that the underlying connection-pool bug has been resolved. Demonstrating functional correctness requires empirical validation. A subsequent passing run of test_waiter_notified_on_return is evidence that the patch fixes the exercised case of the race, while broader task acceptance continues to follow the project’s verification suite. At the network layer, a dropped reply leaves the caller uncertain whether the server committed the edit or failed before processing the payload. The retry and idempotency contracts analyzed in section 7.5 resolve this operational uncertainty.

7.3.4 Rejection diagnostics

If a rejected tool call returns only "Error: operation failed", the model guesses blindly on its next turn. If a permission denial dumps the host directory tree and environment variables to explain why a path was refused, the error message itself leaks unauthorized state. When an operation fails, the runtime must return a structured, bounded diagnostic that isolates the failure condition without leaking information beyond the task’s authorized grants. A structural rejection should identify the missing expected_version property. A concurrency conflict should state that revision r17 has been superseded. Permission rejections, by contrast, must refuse access cleanly without exposing directory structures or path hints that an agent might probe. The runtime logs whether a rejection originated locally during client-side schema validation or remotely at the service endpoint, allowing downstream decision logic to distinguish between an unattempted proposal and a failed remote mutation. Clear, bounded diagnostics give the model the exact evidence needed to synthesize a valid revision, which must then traverse the same validation pipeline.

The descriptor, its layered checks, and its diagnostics together define one tool’s contract, and every check so far has assumed that the runtime already holds that contract and knows which endpoint implements it. In a real deployment the contract arrives from elsewhere: a repository server, another team’s service, a vendor’s integration. How the runtime learns which operations exist, and why learning that an operation exists confers nothing, is the next problem. How many of the discovered descriptors to stage on each turn is a separate budget question that section 7.9 takes up.

7.4 Interoperable Tool Discovery

In practice, a tool implementation such as conditional_replace_file can live in several environments: an in-process adapter compiled into the application, a standalone background daemon, or a remote microservice managed by another team. Before the model can propose a valid call, the host runtime must determine which operations that endpoint exposes and how to encode their arguments. This discovery phase supplies that interface metadata. Under the grant rule, however, learning that an operation exists confers no authority to execute it. Discovery defines interface syntax, whereas authorization governs execution rights.

7.4.1 The Model Context Protocol

When building a self-contained agent with a small, static set of tools, registering local functions directly in-process is simple and fast. However, when multiple host applications must connect to independently maintained tool services, custom point-to-point integration quickly becomes impractical. The underlying challenge is combinatorial. Without a shared interface, \(\HostRuntimeCount\) host runtimes integrating \(\ToolServiceCount\) tool services require up to \(\HostRuntimeCount \times \ToolServiceCount\) bespoke adapters. Standardizing on a common protocol reduces this burden to \(\HostRuntimeCount + \ToolServiceCount\) implementations, allowing each side to build to the specification once.

A shared protocol turns pairwise adapters into one implementation per side.

Decoupling tools across a protocol boundary also provides fault isolation. When a tool runs in a separate process, a memory leak, segmentation fault, or unhandled blocking call crashes only the tool server rather than tearing down the primary agent orchestration loop. Even so, protocol standardization does not eliminate engineering responsibilities. Integrators must still define tool semantics, audit version compatibility, and enforce security policies. Local in-process plugins and out-of-process tool servers represent deliberate deployment trade-offs rather than mutually exclusive or evolutionary stages.

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) exemplifies this architecture. MCP structures integrations across three distinct entities: a host application (such as an interactive environment or agent runtime), clients that manage connections, and servers that expose capabilities. The host runs one client per connected server, and each client speaks the protocol on the host’s behalf. Servers can run as local child processes or as remote network services. This architectural division clarifies integration responsibilities rather than prescribing a specific execution sandbox (Model Context Protocol 2025).

Each deployment model matches different operational demands. A local child process keeps filesystem operations adjacent to the working copy and simplifies process lifecycle management. Conversely, a remote service can centralize credential storage, connection pooling, and backend access without replicating sensitive configurations across every host machine. Both options, however, introduce distinct failure modes and resource boundaries. An unconfined local process still reaches whatever local filesystem it is granted, while a severed network link can leave an in-flight operation executing invisibly on a remote server. The choice between them follows where resource governance and policy enforcement belong, weighing transport latency against tool execution duration rather than presuming any transport topology is universally superior.

7.4.2 Tool listing and invocation

Connecting a host client to multiple servers raises an immediate routing hazard: if two connected servers both advertise an apply_patch operation and the host dispatches calls by bare function name, an untrusted server can silently shadow the repository’s patch tool. Even though MCP separates discovery from execution through two primary wire operations (tools/list to discover available tool definitions and tools/call for execution3), the host must bind each discovered definition to its specific server instance and local policy before exposing it. On the wire, each definition advertises a unique name, a natural-language description, and a JSON Schema governing accepted arguments; an invocation names the target tool and supplies a matching argument dictionary. These messages establish the mechanics of interface exchange rather than a task’s execution rights. The MCP tools specification (Model Context Protocol 2025) defines these message formats and mandates server-side input validation and access controls.

3 JSON-RPC Request Identifiers: MCP frames these messages as JSON-RPC 2.0 requests, each carrying an id that its response echoes (Model Context Protocol 2025). That identifier does the work of this chapter’s call identifier, matching a reply to its request on one connection. It says nothing about whether a second tools/call with the same arguments is the same intended effect, which is why retry safety needs the separate operation identity developed in section 7.5.

Model Context Protocol. 2025. Model Context Protocol Specification, Version 2025-11-25. Protocol specification.

To trace where policy boundaries sit during execution, consider a repository server advertising two illustrative operations: read_source and apply_patch. A concrete integration sequence proceeds through several verification checkpoints:

  1. The host’s client queries tools/list, and the host admits each returned schema only if its own policy allows it (for example, because the server and its version are on an allowlist), binding each admitted schema explicitly to that server instance and its locally governed tool policy so no other server can shadow it.
  2. The host determines which admitted definitions to expose in the model context for the connection-pool repair task.
  3. When the model proposes an operation, the host runtime validates the arguments against the schema and verifies task-level authorization before transmitting the request.
  4. Upon receiving tools/call, the endpoint independently verifies the request against the underlying operating system permissions and data access controls it manages.

For the connection-pool repair, discovering that apply_patch exists leaves the permitted edit paths and the expected-version preconditions unchanged. The guarantee of atomic, conditional updates derives entirely from the server implementation, never from an encouraging function name. If an endpoint fails to provide conditional-update guarantees, the runtime must reject it as incompatible with the task’s safety contract.

7.4.3 Discovery and authority

Just as an encouraging function name proves nothing about atomicity, a discovered apply_patch description that states “approved for all workspaces” or embeds instructions to steer the model is an untrusted external input, not an authorization grant. A description returned during discovery might accurately summarize an operation, omit critical failure modes, or attempt to redirect model behavior, and even an authenticated server can supply a misleading description because verifying a server’s identity does not imply endorsing every claim it emits.

Filtering which tools appear in the model prompt narrows the attack surface, but catalog filtering alone cannot replace per-call enforcement. Task grants evolve dynamically, and call arguments select specific resources at runtime. Authorizing an agent to inspect src/pool.py does not permit it to read unrelated credentials or transmit private source code to an external service. Defense in depth also requires the server or backing service to enforce its own perimeter rather than assuming every client performs flawless checks. Host authorization and endpoint authorization protect different assets and operational domains; neither supersedes the other. On the return path, protocol framing cleanly differentiates a tool result payload from a system message, but it does not disarm adversarial text within the payload. When ambient credential reach serves as the sole barrier between discovery and side effects, adversarial text in a tool result can steer the agent across repository boundaries (war story 7.1).

↪ Prompt injection delivered through tool responses and other observations is developed in Prompt injection entry points.

War Story 7.1: GitHub MCP private-repository leak (2025)
Context: In May 2025, Invariant Labs demonstrated an attack on an agent connected to GitHub through its MCP integration (Milanta and Beurer-Kellner 2025). The user owned both a public and a private repository and directed the agent, running in a desktop client, to inspect open issues in the public repository. The agent’s GitHub access token held permissions that spanned both repositories.

Mechanism: An attacker filed an issue in the public repository containing prompt instructions designed to redirect the model. After reading the issue as an ordinary tool result, the agent executed the server’s schema-valid tools to inspect the user’s private repositories and then opened a pull request in the public repository containing the exfiltrated contents. Every tool call remained within the personal access token’s broad credential scope, and no runtime check verified whether the target repository matched the task’s intended scope. Although the client prompted users to confirm tool calls by default, developers frequently enable an “Always Allow” setting to avoid operational friction.

Impact: The public pull request disclosed sensitive information, including the private repository name, relocation plans, and salary details. Invariant Labs characterized the breakdown as an architectural failure in agent runtime mediation rather than a software defect in GitHub’s server implementation.

Fix: The researchers recommended enforcing context-aware access control at runtime, such as restricting an agent session to the repository the user named, alongside continuous monitoring of agent-server traffic. They also observed that model alignment training alone failed to stop the attack.

Systems lesson: Discovery, schema validity, and credential authentication all succeeded. The failure was an absence of complete mediation (definition 7.1) against the task’s explicit grants, which should have refused the first private-repository read, a read-only \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) action, well before any write occurred.

Milanta, Marco, and Luca Beurer-Kellner. 2025. GitHub MCP Exploited: Accessing Private Repositories via MCP. Invariant Labs blog.

This exploit succeeded because three distinct conditions coincided:

  1. The agent ingested untrusted external text that mounted an indirect prompt injection, attacker instructions planted in data the agent reads as input (Greshake et al. 2023).
  2. The agent held ambient authority broader than its active task required.
  3. The runtime provided tools capable of public mutations.

Eliminating any single condition breaks the attack chain. The second condition is the classic confused deputy problem (Hardy 1988), which the introduction glossed as the hazard of authority that comes from where a program runs rather than from the request it serves. The tool boundary is the first place the runtime must defeat it mechanically.

↩︎ The confused deputy, glossed alongside zero ambient authority, is established in The fail-plausible fault.

Definition 7.3: Confused deputy

A confused deputy is a program holding legitimate authority for one purpose that is manipulated into exercising that authority on behalf of a party with no right to direct it, because the system does not tie the authority exercised to the party directing the action.

  1. Significance: In an agent system the runtime is the deputy. It holds task credentials and dispatches tool calls, so every prompt injection is an attempt to borrow those privileges, and the authority level assigned to the task, from read-only inspection (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\)) to irreversible external mutation (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\)), caps what a successful injection can cost.
  2. Distinction: The attacker acquires no new credentials; it borrows authority the deputy already holds. Checking the deputy’s identity reveals nothing, because the deputy is legitimate. Authorization must instead check whether this request, within the active task, justifies exercising the deputy’s authority.
  3. Common pitfall: Approving an action because its model-generated rationale looks plausible. A justification such as “required for debugging” is part of the unverified request, not independent evidence that the operation is authorized.

In the GitHub incident, the agent exercised the user’s repository credentials on behalf of the remote attacker who wrote the issue text. Prompt injection and exfiltration channels need defenses of their own, but the tool boundary directly governs the second condition. The runtime defeats the confused deputy by evaluating every call against the task’s explicit grants rather than against the reach of the credential it happens to hold.

Checkpoint 7.1: Discovery is not permission

A repository server advertises a schema-valid apply_patch tool whose description says “use any path needed to complete the repair.” The current task permits edits only to its designated working copy. Later, a read result includes a request to upload repository contents to an external endpoint.

Discovery establishes how to address an operation, and authorization determines whether a given task may attempt it. Neither mechanism, however, resolves whether an in-flight mutation actually took effect when a network connection drops or a response times out. Because permission to initiate an action does not confer permission to blindly repeat it when the outcome is uncertain, robust execution requires explicit operation identity and idempotency guarantees.

7.5 Retrying Uncertain Actions

Permission to initiate the staging restart POST /containers/srv-stage-04/restart does not settle what happened when its socket times out. At that timeout, the client cannot tell whether the request was dropped in transit, delayed in a network queue, still running inside the daemon, halted after partially reconfiguring the service, or completed with only its acknowledgment lost. A client-side timeout4 records only that the caller stopped waiting for a response; it conveys no information about endpoint execution state (Cristian 1991). Deciding what to do next requires two orthogonal pieces of knowledge, the operation’s repeat behavior and evidence about the earlier attempt, and prompting the model to retry supplies neither. Settlement before retry (principle 8) permits repeating an uncertain mutation only after its repetition has been shown safe; otherwise the runtime records the mutation as unresolved.

4 Once or Not at All: The remote-procedure-call guarantee that a failed call executed either once or not at all, met for model calls in What a request specifies, applies unchanged to a timed-out tool call. What changes is the price of the uncertainty: a timed-out generation at worst wastes a bill, whereas a timed-out mutation may already have changed the world. An agent runtime must therefore preserve “once or not at all” as an explicit, unsettled status rather than collapsing it into a conventional failure that invites an immediate retry.

Cristian, Flaviu. 1991. “Understanding Fault-Tolerant Distributed Systems.” Communications of the ACM 34 (2): 56–78. https://doi.org/10.1145/102792.102801.
Definition 7.4: Uncertain outcome

Uncertain outcome is the recorded status of a dispatched operation whose effect the runtime can neither confirm nor exclude, because its reply was lost, late, or never produced. The operation stays unsettled until an endpoint deduplication contract, a reconciliation query, or an authorized decision establishes what happened.

  1. Significance: An unsettled mutation blocks any retry that could duplicate it. Under blind retry, the duplicate rate tracks the reply-loss rate almost one for one (notebook 7.1).
  2. Distinction: A definite failure provides actionable knowledge, whereas an uncertain outcome represents missing information. A network timeout leaves the execution unknown; it does not make it a failure.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording a timed-out restart as a definitive failure, which improperly authorizes the very retry that duplicates the mutation.

7.5.1 Delayed attempts

Suppose the runtime dispatches restart attempt A, the network buffers the packet in transit, and when the client timeout fires, a status probe reports that the container has not restarted. If the runtime treats that unchanged state as proof that attempt A was dropped and dispatches attempt B, attempt B restarts the container first and the delayed attempt A arrives moments later to restart it a second time. A probe that reads an unchanged service state reports the present accurately, yet it cannot guarantee that a delayed request will not execute in the near future.

An unchanged probe cannot rule out an attempt still in flight.

The probe returned accurate telemetry, but the runtime drew a flawed inference, mistaking “not yet observed” for “cannot still happen.” An attempt already executing inside the service produces an identical hazard. Sending an asynchronous cancellation request settles this ambiguity only if the endpoint explicitly confirms what halted and whether any side effects committed or could still commit. Safe repetition therefore demands much more than a client timeout followed by an optimistic read.

7.5.2 Idempotent actions

Setting a configuration field to "timeout_ms": 5000 twice leaves the field at 5000, whereas incrementing a counter, appending a log entry, or restarting a container produces a second event on the second call. Deciding whether to retry an unacknowledged tool call therefore depends on the physical consequence of running the operation a second time, which is captured by whether the operation is idempotent.5

5 Idempotent (Latin idem, “the same,” and potent, “power”): Benjamin Peirce introduced the term in his 1870 survey of linear associative algebras to describe an element that remains unchanged when raised to any positive integer power, \(\IdempotentAlgebraElement^\PositiveIntegerExponent = \IdempotentAlgebraElement\) (Peirce 1881). Systems engineering applies the term to system state, where executing an idempotent mutation multiple times leaves the system in the same state as executing it once. Algebraic idempotence, however, guarantees nothing about identical return payloads, execution costs, or hidden side effects introduced along the execution path.

Peirce, Benjamin. 1881. “Linear Associative Algebra.” American Journal of Mathematics 4 (1): 97–229. https://doi.org/10.2307/2369153.
Definition 7.5: Idempotent action

Idempotent action is an operation whose repeated application has the same effect on a specified state as one application, \(\StateOperation(\StateOperation(\ToolTargetState)) = \StateOperation(\ToolTargetState)\), under the operation’s stated conditions.

  1. Significance: Idempotence is the cheapest settlement. A timed-out idempotent call can be retried without reconciliation, which removes the duplicate-effect path priced in notebook 7.1.
  2. Distinction: The property belongs strictly to the implemented operation and its target state; it does not guarantee identical network responses, zero resource consumption, or isolation from concurrent writes by other processes.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming idempotence from an HTTP verb or tool name alone. Operations such as restarting a service or appending a log entry introduce new state transitions on every invocation, regardless of the API naming convention.

Whether an operation is idempotent depends on the endpoint’s implementation rather than its name or HTTP verb. Table 2 sorts the three repeat semantics at the tool boundary by what a second execution means and what the runtime must still check before allowing one.

The repair’s conditional update, a set-a-desired-state operation in table 2, shows why the classification alone does not settle a timeout. Suppose a repair tool predicates its patch on source version r17. Once it installs r18, repeating the conditional request cannot accidentally overwrite subsequent edits, provided the endpoint checks the version and applies the write atomically. The repeated request returns an optimistic concurrency conflict rather than a success status. While this atomic check protects the repository from corruption, it leaves open the question of whether the earlier attempt or a competing process committed r18. Resolving that attribution requires tracking operation identity across attempts. The endpoint must retain each operation’s result and return it to a repeated request, which is the deduplication contract of section 7.5.3.

Repeat behavior operates along an orthogonal axis from the authority levels \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) through \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\) defined in The H·S·A Exposure Space. An authority level governs whether an agent may execute an action autonomously; repeat behavior dictates whether the runtime can safely re-execute that action after an ambiguous failure. These concerns intersect directly at level \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\), which is reserved for external operations backed by well-defined compensating actions and a separate retry policy preventing duplicate effects. An external tool call qualifies for \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\) autonomy only if an executable compensation handler is in place and the runtime settles every transport timeout prior to retrying, relying on verified idempotent semantics, an endpoint deduplication contract, or bounded reconciliation. The staging restart that opened this chapter is such a call. It is an external mutation that adds an event on every execution, so the remediation agent may run it at \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\) only if that compensation contract is in place and its timeout can be settled by one of those three means.

Napkin Math 7.1: Which retry path duplicates an effect?
Problem: Suppose inbound requests and outbound replies are lost independently, with request-loss probability \(\RequestLossProbability\) and reply-loss probability \(\ReplyLossProbability\), each 2 percent. Every delivered request executes successfully, and every caller timeout triggers exactly one blind retry of a non-idempotent operation without deduplication. Dropped packets are assumed to be permanently lost rather than delayed. What is the probability that a single dispatched action executes more than once, and how many duplicate mutations will an agent fleet induce each day?

Math: The initial attempt times out whenever the outbound request or the returning reply is lost, with probability \(\Pr(\text{timeout}) = 1-(1-\RequestLossProbability)(1-\ReplyLossProbability)\), here \(1-(1-0.02)(1-0.02)\), or 3.96 percent. Producing an unintended duplicate mutation requires a specific sequence. The original request must reach the endpoint, its confirmation reply must be lost in transit, and the subsequent retry request must successfully reach the endpoint, so \(\Pr(\text{dup}) = (1-\RequestLossProbability)\,\ReplyLossProbability\,(1-\RequestLossProbability)\). With the assumed rates:

\(\Pr(\text{duplicate effect}) = (1-0.02)\times 0.02\times(1-0.02)\)

This evaluates to 1.9208 percent. Whether the retry’s own confirmation reaches the caller has no bearing on whether the endpoint mutated twice. In a fleet of 50 workers where each worker executes 4,000 mutating calls per day, the system processes 200,000 daily calls. Under this naive retry policy, the fleet triggers approximately 3,842 duplicate mutations every day.

Systems insight: The duplicate execution rate tracks the reply-loss rate almost one-to-one because creating a duplicate requires losing a reply between two successfully delivered requests. Losing an initial request produces only an unexecuted, harmless timeout. While halving reply loss reduces duplicate mutations by half, implementing an endpoint deduplication contract eliminates this failure mode entirely. At production scale, protocol-level deduplication contracts, not network hardening, serve as the primary reliability control. This simplified model ignores delayed packets, which introduce additional duplicate execution paths.

7.5.3 Operation identity and endpoint contracts

If a runtime derives deduplication keys by hashing the tool name and arguments, a deliberate second restart of srv-stage-04 an hour later collides with the first and is silently skipped. If it generates a fresh key on every timeout, every retry executes as a brand-new restart. A logical operation ID, commonly called an idempotency key in transactional storage and payment systems, identifies a single intended effect across multiple transport attempts. As Pat Helland observed, any distributed messaging substrate that survives network partitions and process crashes must occasionally retransmit packets. Consequently, the receiving endpoint, rather than the transport network, must assume responsibility for rendering duplicate messages harmless (Helland 2012). An attempt ID, in contrast, tracks an individual request-response exchange over the wire. When the agent runtime retries an unacknowledged restart, it preserves the original operation ID while generating a fresh attempt ID for transport tracing. When the agent later issues a distinct request to restart the same container, the runtime allocates a new operation ID even though the arguments are identical.

An operation identifier provides safety only when governed by an explicit endpoint contract. This contract binds the operation ID to the caller’s identity, the target resource scope, and the serialized payload. If a client reuses an active operation ID with mismatched arguments, the endpoint must reject the request with a client error (such as an HTTP 409 Conflict) rather than silently dispatching a mutated operation. The endpoint must also enforce access control independently on each attempt, because possessing a valid operation token does not substitute for authorized credentials when dispatching actions or retrieving retained execution results.

The contract must also govern concurrent and interleaved requests. If a duplicate request arrives while the original attempt is actively executing, the endpoint should return an in-progress or pending status instead of spawning a parallel worker. If a duplicate arrives after execution finishes, the endpoint returns the previously computed and retained result. To uphold this guarantee under crash failures, the endpoint’s persistence mechanism must atomically link the side effect with its stored completion record. If a worker applies the mutation and crashes before persisting the result, a simple log of request receipt cannot prove that re-execution is safe. Where the underlying resource supports it, enclosing both the mutation and the result record within a single database transaction guarantees atomic state transitions. When acting against external services lacking transactional boundaries, the endpoint must coordinate with the downstream system’s native deduplication mechanisms. Every request therefore ends in one of four outcomes of the endpoint’s lookup, and only an unseen operation ID executes (figure 2).

Listing 3 illustrates the client runtime’s implementation of this protocol. The runtime assigns the operation ID once upon admitting the intended mutation, then generates a fresh attempt ID for every network transmission. If repeated retries exhaust the attempt budget, the function returns an explicit unresolved outcome rather than propagating an uncaught exception. This design ensures that transport uncertainty surfaces directly to downstream reconciliation logic rather than disappearing into an unhandled error path. Because this retry loop is safe only when the remote endpoint guarantees deduplication, the runtime invokes this path exclusively for tools whose descriptors formally declare compliance with the operation ID contract.

↪ Recording each admitted operation and its identity durably before dispatch, so that recovery can find every effect that may have begun, is developed in Durable Intent Before Dispatch.

Operation IDs deduplicate retries of one intent by one caller. A different hazard arises when two copies of the caller both believe they own the work, as happens when the runtime restarts or hands a trajectory to a replacement worker while the original is only stalled. Distributed schedulers commonly coordinate such assignments through leases, time-bounded claims on granted authority. If a worker encounters an unobserved execution stall (such as a lengthy garbage collection pause or network blip), its lease may expire. The orchestrator reassigns the task to a second worker while the first worker remains active, creating a split-brain scenario where both workers execute mutations concurrently. Sizing lease durations well above the p99 of observed execution latency reduces the frequency of overlapping executions, but statistical padding cannot eliminate the failure mode entirely. True mutual exclusion requires fencing at the destination resource, such as associating every task reassignment with a monotonically increasing epoch number that the destination endpoint verifies on every write before committing state changes.6 Operation IDs and fencing are complementary: the first makes a repeated request harmless, and the second refuses a request from a caller that no longer owns the work.

6 Epoch Checks at the Resource: Distributed lock services encountered this exact failure mode when stale lock holders continued mutating storage after their leases lapsed. The Chubby lock service addressed this with a sequencer: an opaque token containing the lock name, mode, and generation number, which clients transmit alongside mutating requests and target servers validate before committing writes (Burrows 2006). The check sits at the resource because a stale worker cannot detect its own staleness, so only the mutating resource can reject an out-of-date epoch number.

Burrows, Mike. 2006. “The Chubby Lock Service for Loosely-Coupled Distributed Systems.” USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI).

↪ Leases, fencing tokens, and the handoff where a stale owner’s delayed write meets the new fence are collected for reference in Leases and Fencing.

A deduplication contract is incomplete without an explicit retention window. Endpoints must publish how long they retain operation IDs and cached results, as well as how they handle delayed requests that arrive after that retention boundary has elapsed. Once the retention window expires, resubmitting an old operation ID risks being treated as a brand-new request, reintroducing duplicate-execution risks. An idempotency token prevents duplicate execution only when paired with active endpoint deduplication, durable coupling between side effects and result records, and an unambiguous retention schedule. The tool runtime must never assume stronger guarantees than the underlying interface explicitly provides.

7.5.4 Reconciliation

When an endpoint lacks native deduplication and a mutating call times out, merely observing that the target system now matches the desired state does not prove that the timed-out call produced it. A concurrent agent or human operator might have made the change, or an earlier mutation might already have been overwritten. To settle ambiguous operations against such endpoints, the runtime relies on reconciliation, an authorized, bounded inspection of external system state. Obtaining an operation-specific receipt (such as an execution log entry or transaction identifier bearing the caller’s unique metadata) yields stronger evidence than a bare state check. Runtime diagnostics must keep that epistemic distinction intact rather than synthesizing a false success receipt for an unverified call.

Reconciliation works reliably only when designed prior to dispatch. A mutating tool operating over an endpoint without deduplication can be formalized as a three-part interface:

\[\ReconcilableToolInterface = \langle \text{actuate}(\MutatingToolAction),\ \text{probe}(\MutatingToolAction, \ToolTargetState_0),\ \text{compensate}(\MutatingToolAction) \rangle \tag{1}\]

In equation 1, \(\text{actuate}(\MutatingToolAction)\) performs the desired mutation \(\MutatingToolAction\). The verification probe \(\text{probe}(\MutatingToolAction, \ToolTargetState_0)\) is an authorized read operation, authored alongside the tool, that evaluates whether the intended postcondition holds relative to an initial baseline state \(\ToolTargetState_0\) captured immediately before dispatch. The optional compensation routine \(\text{compensate}(\MutatingToolAction)\) provides an authorized procedure for mitigating partial or unintended side effects if execution falters.7 The probe must be implemented ahead of time as part of the tool contract, because allowing an agent model to improvise verification queries on the fly after a timeout replaces transport uncertainty with unverified heuristic guesswork. Capturing the baseline state \(\ToolTargetState_0\) before dispatch provides the reference point. For example, before appending a configuration block to a remote file, the adapter records the file’s initial length and cryptographic hash. After a timeout, the probe can distinguish whether the current bytes match the baseline, match the expected appended content, or match neither. These observations do not establish which actor produced the current state or whether the original attempt can still execute; those questions require an operation-specific receipt or endpoint guarantee.

7 Compensating Actions: A compensation, the saga mechanism the introduction glossed for \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_2\) grants, is a new forward action that semantically neutralizes an earlier step rather than rolling it back in place. Processing a payment refund, for instance, leaves both the original charge and the subsequent credit in the account’s transaction history. For this reason, tool architectures must treat compensation as a separate authorized operation requiring its own execution budget and outcome settlement, rather than pretending the initial mutation never occurred.

Observing an “unchanged” probe result justifies an immediate retry only when backed by definitive structural guarantees. The runtime must either verify that the original attempt can no longer execute (for example, because the attempt has expired under a published request-expiry rule), or rely on inherently idempotent semantics where repeat execution is harmless. Without those assurances, the delayed-attempt hazard illustrated in section 7.5.1 remains fully active. Reconciliation probes are also distributed network calls that can time out, fail, or exceed their allotted latency budgets, leaving the state unresolved.

Under these conditions, the only sound engineering decision is to classify the action as an unsettled outcome. The runtime preserves the operation identity alongside all gathered telemetry, suppresses automated retries, and escalates the ambiguity to an administrative reconciliation pipeline or human operator. A partially applied mutation frequently requires specialized remediation; many real-world actions lack mathematical inverses, and compensation remains an independent mutation with its own potential failure modes. Downstream tasks that depend strictly on the uncertain operation must block until settlement completes, whereas independent parallel subtasks can safely continue executing.

Checkpoint 7.2: Settling an uncertain effect

Whether an operation ends completed, pending, or permanently ambiguous, that disposition must reach the next model call as a concise, accurate observation, so the agent plans from what is known rather than from an invented outcome. Getting any result back into the model, even the unremarkable output of a test run that did finish, raises a resource problem of its own.

7.6 Observation Stream Truncation

An automated repair agent running a test suite may encounter a process that terminates with a definitive exit code while generating a log far too vast to fit within the subsequent model request. Conversely, a remote network operation may time out with an uncertain execution state while its local connection layer still captures valuable diagnostic telemetry. Determining the concrete execution outcome of a tool invocation and deciding what subset of telemetry to stage into the prompt are distinct systems responsibilities. An agent runtime must maintain this architectural separation rather than collapsing nuanced execution states into an opaque success or failure string.

Tool execution telemetry encounters two distinct resource boundaries on its return path. Capture governs the physical resources (kernel memory, disk storage, CPU cycles, and wall-clock time) spent receiving output from an external process or service. Context staging governs the logical volume of tokens admitted into the model’s prompt after filtering, normalization, and serialization. A brief shell command such as grep -rn or an unconstrained compiler build can emit hundreds of megabytes in seconds. Without rigorous capture limits, the runtime risks buffer overruns, pipe deadlocks, or host memory exhaustion long before prompt construction begins.

↩︎ The context budget that bounds the observation tokens admitted to each request is established in The Working-Set Decision.

7.6.1 Bounded output capture

Waiting for a child process to exit before reading its standard output stalls the child indefinitely as soon as its output exceeds the operating system pipe buffer (64 KB on modern Linux kernels). Buffering all output into host memory instead lets a runaway producer exhaust RAM in seconds. An execution adapter must continuously drain its communication channels while enforcing capture limits through bounded in-memory buffers and quota-managed disk storage.

The canonical failure mode in tool execution is a pipe deadlock, which establishes a circular wait8 across two bounded pipes. An adapter communicating with a child process over standard input and standard output manages two unidirectional kernel pipes. Suppose the host writes a large payload to the child’s input pipe and defers reading standard output until writing completes. If the child process emits verbose startup diagnostics before consuming its input, the output pipe fills, causing the child’s write call to block. Concurrently, the host fills the input pipe buffer, causing its own write call to block. Both processes sleep interruptibly while waiting for the other to read, halting forward progress until an external watchdog timer terminates the task. Resolving the deadlock requires structural decoupling. The adapter must set all file descriptors to non-blocking mode and multiplex reads and writes across an asynchronous event loop, or dedicate concurrent worker threads to drain standard output and standard error independently. Draining output streams must never block on transmitting input.

8 Circular Wait: Coffman, Elphick, and Shoshani identified four conditions that must all hold for a deadlock: mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, and a circular chain of processes each waiting on the next (Coffman et al. 1971). Two full pipes satisfy all four, since each side holds its own buffer, cannot take the other’s, and waits on it. A readiness loop removes the circular wait, because the host never blocks on one channel while the other has bytes to move.

Coffman, Edward G., Michael Elphick, and Arie Shoshani. 1971. “System Deadlocks.” ACM Computing Surveys 3 (2): 67–78. https://doi.org/10.1145/356586.356588.

An operational capture policy must also define explicit behavior when limits are reached. When incoming data exceeds a retention threshold, the runtime can either drop subsequent bytes while continuing to drain the pipe to prevent process stalls, or actively terminate the executing process. The runtime must persist telemetry recording which policy was triggered and whether byte loss occurred. Because continuously reading and discarding gigabytes of runaway output still burns CPU cycles and context-switch overhead, an output-volume limit complements execution timeouts and kernel resource controls (such as POSIX process quotas or cgroup limits) rather than replacing them. Process termination is itself an asynchronous state transition with its own failure semantics, addressed alongside cancellation mechanisms in section 7.8.4.

Within these capture boundaries, the runtime extracts the most informative evidence for model evaluation. For software engineering and compilation workloads, preserving an initial prefix and a trailing suffix captures the critical context. Build systems print environment configuration, command invocations, and compilation flags at the start, while emitting panic stack traces, assertion failures, and summary verdicts at the very end. However, intermediate warnings or early test failures can easily be lost in the omitted middle. A production capture policy must therefore disclose the exact magnitude of its omissions, while specialized diagnostic filters can selectively extract recognized error patterns from the middle stream. Listing 4 illustrates why naive head-only truncation fails in automated workflows: in this representative test log, the panic trace and assertion failure appear only after tens of thousands of passing test cases.

Under a head-only policy, the model receives a nonzero exit status with zero explanatory context, forcing it to guess at root causes rather than making informed repairs. A head-and-tail policy resolves this by partitioning the observation byte budget \(\ObservationByteBudget\) across three components:

\[\ObservationByteBudget = \HeadByteAllocation + \NoticeByteAllocation + \TailByteAllocation \tag{2}\]

In equation 2, \(\HeadByteAllocation\) reserves space for the initial execution prefix, \(\NoticeByteAllocation\) allocates bytes for an explicit truncation disclaimer, and \(\TailByteAllocation\) preserves the most recent output suffix. The adapter implements this with fixed memory overhead: \(\HeadByteAllocation\) bytes are captured into a linear buffer until full, after which incoming bytes feed a ring buffer of size \(\TailByteAllocation\). The omission counter increases only when a byte is evicted from that ring, so bytes still retained in the tail are not counted as dropped. When the child process exits, the adapter serializes the head buffer, an omission notice detailing the exact count of dropped bytes, and the ring buffer in chronological order. Capture memory stays fixed however long the process writes (figure 3).

Line counts are convenient for display, but they offer no memory safety because a single line can span gigabytes. The runtime therefore enforces byte limits across all capture and parser buffers, and the ingestion layer truncates on valid Unicode code-point boundaries so that multi-byte characters are never split before token lengths are measured. Because the model context is billed and bounded in tokens rather than bytes, an assumed bytes-per-token ratio serves only to size \(\ObservationByteBudget\) in advance. Once the observation is staged, the runtime measures it with the model’s own tokenizer against the observation share \(\ObservationTokenCount\) of the context budget. That token count includes schema framing, invocation provenance, exit status fields, and the truncation notice itself, leaving headroom for task history, system instructions, and generation so that tool output cannot crowd out the rest of the window.

Napkin Math 7.2: The cost of carrying one observation through a trajectory
Problem: A hypothetical test log contains 24 MB of text. Assume, only for this estimate, 3 bytes per token. The model’s context window is 128,000 tokens. The adapter instead produces a 4,000-token serialized observation, including its metadata, and the context policy selects that same payload for 30 later model calls. Assume input costs $3 per million uncached tokens and cached input costs 10 percent of that price. What are the size and later input-cost consequences?

Math: Dividing the raw bytes by the assumed bytes per token gives \(24 \times 10^{6} / 3 = 8{,}000{,}000\) tokens, or 62.5× the entire window. The raw log cannot be admitted to this request. Actual tokenization depends on the specific vocabulary and input encoding; a coarse byte ratio serves for capacity planning rather than admission validation.

The shaped result occupies 3.1 percent of the whole window, before accounting for other context and output reserve. Its later input contribution is \(4{,}000 \times 30 = 120{,}000\) tokens. At the assumed prices, these later reads cost $0.36 if all are uncached, or $0.036 if all qualify for cached-input billing. These figures exclude the initial inclusion, output generation, and all other request content.

Systems insight: Every staged observation incurs repeated token fees across subsequent turns that retain it in context. Its carried input is \(\ShapedObservationSize \cdot \RetainingCallCount\) tokens. This is the serialized size \(\ShapedObservationSize\) of this one observation (one contributor to the observation share \(\ObservationTokenCount\) of each request) times the number \(\RetainingCallCount\) of later calls that retain it, and systems engineers control both factors. Halving the staged payload or pruning the observation once the agent acts on its diagnostic cuts token expenditure proportionally, while cached-input billing cuts the carried cost by a factor of 10 under the assumed prices. Aggressive observation shaping directly lowers the amortized inference cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), provided the retained evidence remains sufficient for subsequent reasoning.

↩︎ The cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), is established in Reliability and Cost over a Horizon.

7.6.2 Truncation notices and inspection handles

Once a head-and-tail policy drops the middle of a log to fit \(\ObservationByteBudget\), the model needs to know whether those omitted bytes still sit in storage or were discarded at the pipe boundary. Otherwise it cannot tell whether to request the missing slice or re-run the test suite with a narrower filter. A truncated observation must therefore state what was selected, what was omitted, and whether the missing data remains retrievable, distinguishing a complete capture that was staged as an excerpt from an unrecoverable drop at the pipe. Omission figures must also reflect what the adapter actually measured: if a stream dropped packets or ended early under buffer overruns, the runtime reports that uncertainty rather than fabricating an exact byte count.

When storage policy permits retention, the runtime can spool the full raw output to an external artifact store and supply the model with an inspection handle, the raw-payload handle of The Working-Set Decision as it appears at the tool boundary, subject to the same handle rule. Instead of re-executing an expensive build or test run to view omitted output, the agent can issue secondary queries such as read_observation_slice(handle, offset_bytes, max_bytes) to examine targeted slices. It can also invoke a server-side regular-expression search over the retained log that returns only matching lines and their byte offsets. Retaining raw logs outside the context window shifts the engineering burden to storage governance.
