Because retained logs consume disk space and often contain environment variables, authorization tokens, or internal paths, the artifact store must enforce capacity quotas and eviction horizons alongside the same disclosure policies that govern the primary tool call. It must return an explicit error when a handle has expired or been evicted. Content hashes can verify that a stored log has not changed, whereas read authorization and retention lifetime remain separate runtime checks.

Structured tools can avoid post-hoc log slicing altogether by partitioning query results into pages bounded in both record count and serialized byte size, so that a single oversized record cannot overflow the response. Each page reports whether more records remain, whether a scan limit or deadline cut the search short, and which cursor resumes the query. Because authorization and snapshot isolation are independent of pagination, the contract must also state whether later pages read from a frozen snapshot or see concurrent writes.

↩︎ The result envelope’s completion status and continuation cursor are established in The result envelope.

The placement of continuation state highlights a key systems trade-off amplified by agentic workloads: stateful server-side cursors vs. stateless client tokens. A stateful cursor maintains an open database cursor, transaction snapshot, or cache entry on the server until the client finishes iterating. However, autonomous agents abandon search trajectories routinely. An informative diagnostic on the first page often redirects the agent’s entire plan, causing abandoned server sessions to accumulate until garbage collection or time-to-live timeouts reclaim them. A stateless cursor avoids server-side state by encoding the continuation position directly within the token passed to the client. Because that token travels through the model’s context, where generated or injected text could alter it to reach rows outside the original query, the backend authenticates it. It serializes a query descriptor, the last evaluated sort key, and an issuance timestamp into a payload \(\StatelessCursorPayload\) and returns the token \(\StatelessCursorPayload \parallel \text{MAC}_{\text{key}}(\StatelessCursorPayload)\) signed with a secret server key. When the client returns the token, the backend verifies the message authentication code and expiration time, ensuring resistance against client-side tampering without consuming server memory. The trade-offs for stateless cursors are relaxed consistency and increased prompt overhead. Resumption queries typically evaluate against live data rather than an isolated snapshot, and serialized cryptographic tokens consume prompt tokens on every turn that carries them.

Bounded capture and disciplined staging keep tool output within its memory and context budgets without deadlocking the child. Fitting the bytes into a budget, however, says nothing about whether they faithfully represent what the program displayed or what the process actually did. A terminal log can be garbled by its own redraws, and a clean-looking log can come from a run that checked nothing.

7.7 Terminal Output Normalization

When a command-line tool executes interactively, it redraws progress indicators with carriage returns and styles diagnostics with terminal escape sequences. Captured directly at the byte level, standard output does not represent the clean visual layout a human engineer sees on a terminal screen. Wherever possible, tool adapters should invoke command-line utilities in noninteractive or batch modes (for example, --batch or --no-color), or ideally request structured machine-readable outputs such as JSON or JUnit XML. Structured reports provide discrete test identifiers, execution timings, and explicit assertions, eliminating the brittle heuristics required to parse colored progress logs. Even so, structured formatting is a transport convention; receiving a syntactically valid JSON payload does not guarantee that the tool performed all required checks or that its outputs can be trusted without schema validation and memory bounds.

When terminal output is unavoidable, the adapter must enforce a declared normalization policy backed by a stateful virtual terminal emulator or bounded screen buffer. Removing ANSI color sequences (\x1b[31m) is straightforward, but naively stripping control bytes like carriage returns (\r) or cursor positioning sequences silently corrupts output semantics.9 Because a carriage return moves the cursor to the beginning of the line without advancing to the next, a terminal progress indicator updates by repeatedly overwriting existing text. Stripping \r without simulating terminal geometry causes overwritten states to concatenate. For the bytes Running... \rFailed!, the screen shows Failed! written over the start of the line (Failed!...), whereas naive stripping yields Running... Failed!, a line that never appeared. Over a long run the same error accumulates megabytes of repeated progress fragments. The adapter’s parser must therefore maintain cursor coordinates across chunked streaming reads while bounding its internal buffer allocations. When an adapter supports only a subset of control sequences, it must explicitly mark unsupported or incomplete escape sequences rather than discarding them silently. Because collapsing interactive updates into a final snapshot loses intermediate state transitions, the runtime should retain the raw byte stream in secondary storage as an authoritative audit log while delivering only the normalized, bounded representation to the model context. Normalization also shifts byte positions, so the adapter records a mapping from normalized offsets back to raw offsets to keep range queries through an inspection handle aligned with the original text.

9 Terminal Control Functions: The escape sequences that color and reposition terminal text were standardized in ECMA-48, first published in 1976 and adopted internationally as ISO/IEC 6429 (Ecma International 1991). Its control functions act on a presentation position on a display rather than on a stream of lines, which is why recovering what an operator saw requires emulating that display rather than filtering bytes.

Ecma International. 1991. Control Functions for Coded Character Sets. ECMA-48, 5th edition. Ecma International.

Tool adapters must preserve the provenance of standard output (stdout) and standard error (stderr) while treating stream assignments as advisory conventions. In Unix environments, programs frequently emit progress updates, debugging traces, or configuration warnings across either descriptor, and capturing the two streams through separate pipes forfeits their relative temporal interleaving. When chronological event ordering is essential for root-cause diagnosis, adapters should multiplex streams using pseudo-terminal (pty) pairs or timestamped chunk logging. In extracting diagnostics, the adapter should rely on known output formats such as compiler error lines and language tracebacks, retaining source file paths and line offsets while reporting parsing failures explicitly rather than guessing a root cause from an incomplete fragment. Normalizing terminal output cleans up presentation, but the resulting text carries no intrinsic authority. Safe serialization practices, including escaping control characters and enforcing UTF-8 boundaries, protect the agent’s structural messaging format from delimiter injection, while the evidence-scope rule continues to dictate what the text actually proves.

7.7.1 Process status and evidential scope

Even a cleanly normalized log cannot by itself prove that a check ran. A test runner pointed at an unmatched directory filter discovers zero tests and exits cleanly with status 0. A shell pipeline run without set -o pipefail suffers a fatal crash in its first command while still returning status 0, because its final formatting stage succeeded. The adapter must therefore read process disposition directly from operating-system primitives, such as waitpid status macros or container runtime lifecycle events, rather than parsing success messages from output logs. It must still treat exit status 0 as an application convention rather than proof that every required check ran. Querying the process supervisor lets the adapter report an unambiguous exit code,10 an unhandled terminating signal, or a timeout without textual speculation. Equally, observing end-of-file on standard streams does not prove that a process has finished its work, because a daemon or worker process can close its inherited descriptors while continuing to run in the background. Conversely, a broken socket or interrupted channel leaves the state of any requested side effects uncertain.

10 Eight-Bit Exit Status: Under POSIX semantics, a parent process inspects child termination through status macros like WIFEXITED and WEXITSTATUS, which yield only the low-order eight bits (values 0–255) of the argument passed to exit. A program that terminates with exit code 256 wraps modulo 256 and appears to the parent as exit status 0. Standard shells also synthesize return codes above 128 when a process is killed by a signal (for example, 137 for SIGKILL 9 or 139 for SIGSEGV 11), collapsing fatal OS signals into ambiguous numerical exit statuses. An adapter that records explicit disposition variants, such as exited(code) vs. signaled(sig), avoids truncating or conflating these conditions.

An API client can likewise catch an internal runtime exception, print the error payload to standard output, and terminate with code 0 because the transport exchange itself finished without an unhandled crash. Under the evidence-scope rule, the adapter treats the operating-system process status as one evidentiary signal alongside structured output payloads and the task’s acceptance criteria. In a connection-pool repair, for instance, confirming that a targeted reproduction test passes on workspace revision r18 proves only that the reproducer succeeded; it does not substantiate broader system stability or justify full patch acceptance. Listing 5 illustrates how an adapter packages these signals together. The adapter runs a targeted test against workspace snapshot r18, parses its assertions, and captures the full output stream within its retention budget, while staging only a bounded summary into the prompt context.

Each block in listing 5 answers a separate question so the model cannot mistake a clean exit or a short excerpt for full verification. The call_id field ties the result to the turn that requested it. The scope field pins the evidence to workspace revision r18, so a pass on one commit cannot be credited to a later edit. The check field records that the single reproducer passed while broader regression suites were not run. Because the full log was captured but only the header and summary verdict were staged into the prompt, the observation block marks capture as complete and staged_view as an excerpt, and it exposes obs_42 as an inspection handle for paginated follow-up reads. Had resource limits truncated collection at the pipe, capture itself would be marked incomplete with the surviving byte ranges noted. Under the grant rule, the runtime mediates every read on obs_42, so knowing the handle string confers no ambient authority across sessions.11

11 Names vs. Capabilities: Dennis and Van Horn’s foundational capability model granted authority through possession of an unforgeable ticket or reference (Dennis and Van Horn 1966). Agent runtime architectures intentionally stop short of treating context-visible observation handles as capabilities. Because text in a model’s context window can be logged, leaked, copied by prompt injections, or replayed across user boundaries, the runtime treats an inspection handle merely as an identifier (a name). The runtime verifies caller authorization and execution scope on each access, ensuring that possessing a handle string grants no ambient authority.

Dennis, Jack B., and Earl C. Van Horn. 1966. “Programming Semantics for Multiprogrammed Computations.” Communications of the ACM 9 (3): 143–55. https://doi.org/10.1145/365230.365252.
Checkpoint 7.3: Interpreting bounded observations

Synchronous tool execution assumes the adapter can resolve process status, normalize terminal streams, and package evidence within the span of a single model turn. When operations run longer than conversational deadlines allow, asynchronous dispatch extends this same return-path contract to running jobs, tracking process lifecycles and staging bounded observations without mistaking dispatch for completion.

7.8 Asynchronous Tool Dispatch

When an automated repair agent launches an extensive regression test suite, it rarely has any useful decision to make until the results arrive. A prompt return from that tool invocation is only an acknowledgment of receipt; the actual computation has only just begun. During the intervening minutes or hours, the agent runtime must supervise background execution, enforce resource budgets, and maintain system safety without freezing its control plane or burning inference tokens on idle waiting.

7.8.1 Synchronous and asynchronous interfaces

Choosing between blocking on a call and returning a background handle trades control-plane responsiveness against workspace isolation: blocking a supervisor thread on a five-minute integration build leaves the host unable to answer cancellation signals, whereas returning a handle immediately lets the agent edit src/pool.py midway through the test run and corrupt the workspace snapshot under test. A synchronous tool interface, which blocks the caller until execution finishes and returns the final result directly, fits sub-second database lookups or local checks where the agent cannot proceed without the answer, though worker threads must still monitor process heartbeats, stream partial output, and enforce timeouts without freezing the control plane. An asynchronous interface instead decouples job submission from job completion by returning a handle representing the pending task alongside methods to inspect its status, stream intermediate observations, or await termination. This separation allows the agent trajectory to suspend cleanly without burning model inference cycles, or to continue executing independent tasks concurrently, provided the runtime isolates the background job within a fixed snapshot or copy-on-write workspace so concurrent edits cannot contaminate the run.

Decoupling task execution from model inference also resolves the serving side of the tool-wait problem named in The six phases of a turn. While a background job runs, the model generates no tokens, so the serving engine can keep, move, or discard and later recompute the trajectory’s KV cache state under the paused-state policies of Paused-State Policies, independently of the tool’s lifecycle. Keeping an open HTTP connection or a blocked synchronous socket throughout a long build provides no caching guarantee and only ties up host network resources. When the background job completes and the trajectory resumes, the runtime constructs the next prompt, and the serving engine either recomputes attention state over the prompt prefix or restores it from a prefix cache, depending on what it has evicted.

7.8.2 Job handles

Once job submission is decoupled from completion, the handle returned at dispatch must keep a queued job distinct from a finished one. If an asynchronous test runner returns "status": "ok" as soon as a job enters the work queue, the model treats a queued test suite as a passing one. If a later status poll times out and the adapter reports "status": "failed", the model rolls back a patch while the test job is still running in the background. The runtime admits a proposed asynchronous call using the same validation, authorization, and resource checks required for synchronous invocation. If the executor accepts the work, the runtime records the job’s identity, owner, requested operation, applicable source revision, execution limits, and observation location. A model-visible job handle identifies that record without exposing the executor’s internal process-management interface. Under the principle of least privilege, which gives each component only the authority its current task needs, status reads, output inspection, interactive input, and cancellation each require their own explicit authorization checks. A provider-neutral dispatch acknowledgment records only the pending handle:

{
  "call_id": "call_tests_1",
  "status": "accepted_pending",
  "job_id": "job_tests_18",
  "source_revision": "r18",
  "result_available": false
}

Here call_id correlates the dispatch exchange, while job_id identifies the accepted work for later interactions. If a mutating operation also requires a logical operation ID for retry handling, that identity retains the idempotency semantics established in section 7.5; a job ID alone supplies no deduplication guarantee. Dropping the dispatch reply across an unreliable network leaves the runtime uncertain whether the executor accepted the task, mirroring the ambiguity of a timeout during a synchronous call.

This initial acknowledgment confirms only that the executor accepted the request into its work queue; it makes no claims about whether the process has started running or whether tests have passed. Subsequent status queries can report active execution, an explicit request for user input, completion with a structured result, or failure with diagnostic details. If network partitions or host restarts make the executor temporarily unreachable, returning “last observed running” alongside “current status unavailable” preserves operational context instead of triggering a mistaken rollback.

When the result becomes available, the runtime delivers the bounded observation contract, specifying the tested revision, the checks actually executed, the process exit disposition, and any output truncation. For example, a completed test job at revision r18 can report that test_waiter_notified_on_return passed. Because a child process can exit cleanly while an output buffer truncates under heavy logging, process termination and output completeness remain distinct fields in the observation contract.

7.8.3 Resumption mechanisms

Even when a job handle reports pending state accurately, letting the model poll a five-minute background job every five seconds spends 59 full model calls reading the same 32,768-token context only to receive "still running". Systems track asynchronous job state through three primary mechanisms, distinguished by whether state detection requires model inference (table 3).

By delegating health checks to the runtime, systems avoid redundant inference calls while retaining simple polling semantics. Event-driven notifications (such as webhooks or message queues) further eliminate polling traffic between the runtime and the executor, though they introduce operational complexity. The runtime must authenticate callbacks, correlate incoming events with job identifiers, and handle dropped, delayed, or duplicated deliveries. In practice, receiving a completion notification often acts as a trigger for the runtime to execute a validated status read, rather than treating the notification payload itself as authoritative proof of completion. Both polling and notification architectures require dedicated supervisory resources to manage pending jobs across runtime host failures.

↪ The durable return path that lets a replacement worker recover a pending job’s result after the runtime host fails is developed in Effect and charge reconciliation.

Napkin Math 7.3: The extra cost of asking whether a job finished
Problem: A background test job completes in 300 s. Under a model-directed polling strategy, the agent queries status every 5 s, starting one interval after dispatch and ending strictly before completion. Assume each status check finishes before the next scheduled query and does not alter overall execution time. Each model invocation consumes a 32,768-token prompt (including preceding status history) and generates a 40-token query, with no additional model acknowledgment required. What incremental inference overhead does this polling loop impose compared to event-driven runtime detection?

Math: Both architectures make the initial dispatch call and the final completion call, so those shared calls are excluded. The polling schedule produces 59 intermediate model invocations, consuming 1,933,312 input tokens and 2,360 generated tokens. The selected context length remains fixed across queries rather than accumulating unbounded history.

Assume 95 percent of input tokens qualify for prompt caching discounts, priced at $0.30 per million tokens vs. $3 per million uncached tokens, with generation billed at $15 per million tokens. Multiplying token counts by their respective rates yields $0.8410 in input charges and $0.0354 in output charges, producing $0.8764 of direct inference overhead per background job.

Systems insight: The overhead is \(\StatusPollCount = \lceil \JobDuration/\FiniteChange \TimeInstant \rceil - 1\) status calls for a job of duration \(\JobDuration\) polled every \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\), each reading \(\SequenceLength\) input tokens and generating \(\OutputLength\). It therefore costs \(\StatusPollCount(\SequenceLength\,\BlendedInputTokenPrice + \OutputLength\,\OutputTokenPrice)\) at per-token input price \(\BlendedInputTokenPrice\) (here the cache-blended price) and output price \(\OutputTokenPrice\). It grows linearly with job duration and inversely with the poll interval. Halving the interval roughly doubles the expenditure, and the interval also sets detection delay: a completion is noticed at most \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) after it happens. Under model-directed polling, cost and worst-case delay therefore trade directly, since halving the cost by doubling \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) doubles the worst-case delay, whereas runtime polling decouples the two because its checks cost no inference. The $0.8764 per pending job is more than twice the $0.36 that carrying the shaped observation of notebook 7.2 through 30 uncached calls cost. It inflates the attempt cost in the numerator of effective cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), paying for status information that the host runtime can monitor at near-zero cost. Systems should delegate routine state tracking to the runtime harness, waking the model only when an incoming observation can change the next decision.

7.8.4 Job supervision and cancellation

Delegating status checks to the runtime also leaves the runtime responsible for supervising the background job while the model trajectory is paused. Throughout the job’s lifetime, the runtime enforces its execution deadline, resource ceilings, output budgets, and permitted external side effects, returning the latest status and a timestamped, truncation-marked output slice whenever a bounded inspection request arrives. If a job’s output stream goes silent, the runtime cannot assume deadlock from silence alone; an explicit status signal or diagnostic probe is needed to tell slow computation apart from blocked I/O awaiting user input or a wedged worker process.

When a tool genuinely awaits interactive input, the runtime should avoid guessing from operating-system process sleep states. Wherever possible, adapters invoke tools in non-interactive modes with complete parameters supplied up front; when interaction cannot be avoided, an explicit adapter event or structured message must declare what prompt the job expects. Before forwarding any input from the agent, the runtime re-checks the job’s state, the input’s meaning, and the session’s grants, because answering "yes" to an interactive migration prompt can trigger schema mutations that exceed a read-only inspection grant.

When a job must be stopped, whether by an explicit cancellation request or an expired deadline, the runtime treats cancellation as an asynchronous control operation with its own intermediate states rather than an instantaneous rollback. After acknowledging the cancellation request, the runtime records what the executor actually reports: the job may stop cleanly, finish before the signal arrives, reject cancellation, or become unreachable with termination unconfirmed. Reclaiming every descendant process is where naive cancellation fails. When a background job runs bash -c "make && pytest" and times out, sending SIGTERM only to the top-level shell kills the shell. It leaves make, compiler workers, and pytest running as orphaned children reparented to PID 1, and they still hold filesystem locks and burn CPU cycles. On a POSIX host, the containment unit for local signaling is instead a process group, which groups all processes reached by a single signal. The execution adapter makes each job the leader of its own process group at spawn time by calling setpgid(0, 0) in the child before exec. It then signals the entire group with SIGTERM first, which gives processes a bounded grace period to flush buffers and release locks, and follows with an uncatchable SIGKILL. Listing 6 implements this escalation pattern and reports one of four verified outcomes. The outcome is exited when the group was gone before any signal, terminated when it stopped during the SIGTERM grace period, killed when it stopped only after SIGKILL, and unconfirmed when members remain after both. If an untrusted process deliberately creates a new session or process group to escape group signaling, the runtime must rely on kernel-level isolation boundaries.

↪ Tearing down processes that detach from the task’s process group, as part of a sandbox’s reset contract, is developed in The reset contract.

Two subtle POSIX semantics explain why group_alive pairs os.waitpid(-pgid, os.WNOHANG) with os.killpg(pgid, 0). First, an exited direct child remains in the kernel process table as a zombie until its parent reaps it, so probing the process group before reaping would report an exited child as still alive. The while loop drains every direct child in pgid that has already exited, stopping as soon as waitpid returns 0 (meaning direct children still exist in the group and none is currently ready to reap) or raises ChildProcessError (meaning no direct children remain). Second, waitpid can see only the runtime’s direct children. If the group leader exits while its grandchildren (make and pytest) are still running, the kernel reparents those grandchildren to init (PID 1), making them invisible to waitpid even though they remain in process group pgid. Sending signal 0 via os.killpg(pgid, 0) delivers no signal, yet asks the kernel whether any live process still belongs to pgid regardless of parent. Reaping zombies first and probing pgid with signal 0 afterward therefore confirms whether the entire process tree has exited.

Even when process termination is confirmed, stopping an execution does not roll back side effects that occurred before the signal arrived. A canceled deployment script may already have mutated external cloud resources, and a canceled test run may leave temporary database tables behind. The final observation emitted by the runtime must record all known side effects, pending cleanup obligations, and ambiguous states. In the system’s execution log, a status of “canceled” confirms that execution halted; it does not signify that the system returned to its original state.

↪ Remediating the partial effects and cleanup obligations an interrupted job leaves behind is developed in The Trajectory Saga Pattern.

An asynchronous interface thus carries the tool contract across time. It acknowledges queued work without stalling the trajectory, keeps resource limits and supervision in force while the model waits, returns bounded observations, and never reports a requested state as a confirmed one. Every mechanism so far has treated one tool at a time and taken that tool’s presence for granted. The remaining decisions concern the whole set: which tools the model sees on a given turn, and how much work each one performs.

7.9 Designing the Active Toolkit

Across the src/pool.py repair, the agent has needed to inspect source files, apply candidate patches, run test suites, and read diagnostics, and how the runtime packages those capabilities into a toolkit can fail at either extreme. If each tool exposes too little functionality per invocation, the model must coordinate low-level state across dozens of turns. If the runtime instead exposes an unconstrained catalog, the context window fills with irrelevant tool schemas that consume input capacity and bloat prefill latency on every turn, and each extra descriptor is one more candidate the model must rule out. Whether those candidates lower selection accuracy for a given model and catalog is an empirical question that catalog evaluation (section 7.9.3) measures rather than assumes. The goal of toolkit design is to expose operations so that the model can choose an appropriate next step without carrying unnecessary definitions or managing avoidable intermediate state.

Two design dimensions govern this trade-off. Granularity determines how much work an individual tool performs, while catalog selection determines which subset of tool definitions the model sees on any given turn, known as the active toolkit. A small catalog can contain either narrowly bounded primitives or a broadly expressive command runner, whereas an enterprise environment registering hundreds of specialized tools must stage them selectively rather than presenting the entire catalog on every turn.

7.9.1 Static and on-demand staging

Serializing all 50 registered tool descriptors into every model call burns 15,000 tokens of context capacity on every turn even when a step needs only six tools, whereas selecting a narrow subset on demand risks omitting the one tool the repair needs next. In static staging, the runtime serializes a fixed set of tool descriptors into every request, keeping available interfaces predictable and eliminating separate discovery steps at the cost of carrying unused schemas on every turn. In on-demand staging, the runtime dynamically selects descriptors as the trajectory unfolds, shrinking the prompt footprint and prefill latency at the risk that an omitted tool forces the agent to spend extra turns locating it.

Selection mechanisms vary widely in implementation complexity. A runtime can employ deterministic task-to-tool mappings, hierarchical namespace menus, keyword matching, dense vector retrieval, or a combination of these approaches. Dense retrieval represents one possible search technique rather than a universal requirement for large catalogs. Any match returned by a retriever is merely a candidate descriptor. A robust systems design provides a bounded fallback path, such as a dedicated tool-search operation or an explicit discovery-failure signal, so that an omitted descriptor does not cause the model to conclude erroneously that the operation cannot be performed.

Staging a descriptor makes its syntactic and semantic contract visible to the model; under the grant rule, staging confers no execution privileges. The runtime must still resolve and authenticate every proposed invocation against registered tool identities and permissions before execution. If a dynamic discovery step returns a modified or re-versioned descriptor, the runtime must reconcile that version before executing it, preventing the model from dispatching against an interface different from the one it was provided.

Tool definitions consume prompt capacity alongside system instructions, source excerpts, previous observations, and generation headroom. Systems engineers must measure the final serialized request, including formatting delimiters, wrapper schemas, and selection metadata, rather than tallying independently tokenized description fragments under the false assumption that token boundaries are free. The following notebook counts the catalog-related input for a single model request.

Selection frees most of the catalog’s input, on every call.

Napkin Math 7.4: The input budget of an active catalog
Problem: How much next-call input capacity does a selected catalog release after counting its additional framing and selection information?

Variables: Suppose a registered catalog has 50 tools. Each complete descriptor contributes 300 tokens, including its share of catalog framing. The alternative stages 6 of the same descriptors plus 400 additional tokens for the selection interface and metadata. These are stipulated contributions within the final serialization, with other request content unchanged. In this scenario the runtime selects the subset without another model call.

Math: Full staging uses \(50 \times 300 = 15{,}000\) tokens. The selected descriptors plus selection overhead use \(6 \times 300 + 400 = 2{,}200\) tokens. The released capacity is therefore \(15{,}000 - 2{,}200 = 12{,}800\) tokens.

Systems insight: Selection pays whenever its overhead is smaller than the unused descriptors it removes, \((\RegisteredDescriptorCount - \SelectedDescriptorCount)\,\ToolDescriptorSize > \SelectionOverheadSize\) for \(\SelectedDescriptorCount\) of \(\RegisteredDescriptorCount\) descriptors of \(\ToolDescriptorSize\) tokens each and \(\SelectionOverheadSize\) tokens of selection overhead. Here that is 400 against 13,200 tokens. Break-even sits at about 1.3 unused descriptors, so the saving grows linearly with catalog size. The released capacity recurs on every call that would have carried the full catalog, so over a trajectory it multiplies by the number of turns, like the carried observation in notebook 7.2. The comparison flips when selection needs its own model call or a missed tool forces rediscovery; those calls belong in the same token count.

Static and selected catalogs also interact differently with prefix caching in serving runtimes. When an inference engine uses radix-tree or hash-based KV cache reuse, maintaining an identical serialized prefix across turns allows the engine to bypass prefill computation for earlier prompt segments. Static staging preserves a stable prompt prefix across calls, maximizing cache reuse as long as earlier conversation state remains intact. Conversely, modifying descriptors or reordering them across turns moves the point of prompt divergence earlier, invalidating downstream cached KV blocks and forcing redundant prefill computation. Caching also changes what selection buys. If the full catalog sits in a cached root billed at 10 percent of the uncached price, as assumed in notebook 7.2, the dollar value of the tokens that selection releases in notebook 7.4 shrinks by the same factor of 10. The released context capacity is unchanged. Once caching applies, selection pays mainly in capacity for task evidence rather than in money. A hybrid strategy applies the change-rate ordering of Staging the Next Invocation to the catalog: an immutable core of common descriptors sits in the stable root of the prompt, and task-specific additions are appended later. Even so, systems designers must not retain obsolete or revoked definitions merely to retain a cache hit, as doing so exposes outdated interfaces to the model. Removing an earlier temporary tool definition also alters the prompt prefix for subsequent turns, invalidating the cached state of the assistant responses and observations that followed it.

↩︎ Radix-tree prefix caching and KV block reuse across prompt turns are established in Prefix Caching Across Turns.

7.9.2 Tool granularity

Whereas staging decides which tools appear on a turn, granularity decides how much operational work each call encapsulates. A file-editing interface built from separate open_file, seek_cursor, write_bytes, and close_file tools forces the model to spend four turns and manage raw file descriptors, cursor offsets, and cleanup across the trajectory only to apply a one-line patch. Classic systems modularity principles apply directly here. Parnas established that effective module boundaries hide design decisions that are subject to change (Parnas 1972). Ousterhout formalized this criterion through deep vs. shallow interfaces, measuring how much implementation complexity an abstraction hides relative to the surface area it exposes (Ousterhout 2018). The same principles hold in agent systems, where tool interfaces designed around an agent’s editing, navigation, and testing patterns raised task success rates on software engineering benchmarks (Yang et al. 2024). A deep interface encapsulates substantial functionality behind a concise contract, whereas a shallow interface exposes almost as much complexity as it implements. Decomposing a file edit into open_file, seek_cursor, write_bytes, and close_file is warranted only when intermediate inspection is an explicit requirement.

Parnas, David L. 1972. “On the Criteria to Be Used in Decomposing Systems into Modules.” Communications of the ACM 15 (12): 1053–58. https://doi.org/10.1145/361598.361623.

A task-level patch operation moves mechanical file manipulation behind the tool implementation, returning a unified diff, a resulting version hash, or an explicit conflict. The conditional-edit contract in section 7.3 illustrates this boundary. However, interface naming alone does not guarantee transactional safety. If an operation advertises all-or-nothing execution, the underlying implementation must enforce that atomicity across its mutation scope and failure modes. When operations span multiple files or distributed endpoints, partial side effects can occur, and the runtime must report those partial outcomes explicitly in the tool observation. Bundling multi-step work into a single tool call reallocates responsibility from model planning to runtime execution, but the underlying systems obligations remain intact. For the same edit, the shallow composition spends four model calls where the task-level operation spends one (figure 4).

Careful toolkit design does not require eliminating all overlapping capabilities. A system can effectively combine a structured search tool that returns abstract syntax tree (AST) matches with an authorized shell runner that supports complex log analysis beyond the search tool’s schema. To prevent model confusion, their descriptors must articulate clear functional boundaries, target inputs, and operational limits. Providing duplicate tools with near-identical names and indistinguishable schemas adds selection noise without expanding capability; in contrast, providing complementary interfaces with distinct observation formats, coverage scopes, or authorization grants offers substantial utility. Table 4 summarizes how these interface styles partition responsibility between the model and runtime.

An argument schema constrains the syntactic format of a request, but it cannot constrain the underlying code execution. A specialized symbol-renaming tool may parse external ASTs, inspect secondary source trees, or execute defective third-party libraries. A narrow signature provides safety only when the implementation and its surrounding execution sandbox enforce that advertised boundary. Conversely, a shell runner’s blast radius is defined by its working directory, operating-system privileges, credential access, and network egress rules. Because the side effects of an expressive shell command cannot be reliably determined by inspecting its arguments, the runtime must rely on containment to enforce isolation.

↪ The isolation envelope that enforces a command’s reach regardless of its arguments is developed in Choosing an Isolation Envelope.

Tool implementations should also adopt defensive defaults that fail visibly. When an agent provides an ambiguous file path or target identifier, rejecting the call with an informative diagnostic is far safer than guessing or silently appending data, as appending is a stateful mutation that can silently duplicate configuration entries. A dry-run mode allows the model to inspect a proposed diff against the current workspace state before committing changes; the runtime must still enforce approval gates and verify preconditions at execution time. These defensive defaults make operational failures interpretable, but engineers should remember that rollback capabilities and high-fidelity simulation are properties of the underlying tool implementation, not inherent guarantees of the interface.

7.9.3 Catalog evaluation

Choosing between those granularity and staging options requires measuring end-to-end trajectories rather than single-step accuracy. Comparing a 12-tool structured catalog against a single run_shell tool by tool-selection accuracy alone makes the shell runner look superior, because the model cannot pick the wrong tool name out of a catalog of one. That comparison hides the unconfined mutations and recovery turns the shell introduces. Evaluating toolkit designs therefore requires holding all other experimental factors constant (the benchmark task distribution, initial workspace snapshots, model checkpoints, generation parameters, token budgets, and verification suites). The trials must also exercise realistic failure modes across the connection-pool repair workload. These include locating relevant source files across deep directory hierarchies, applying patches against concurrently modified buffers, interpreting noisy test failure logs, and recovering when a needed descriptor is omitted from the initial active toolkit. Those trials separate catalog coverage gaps from model selection errors, and separate selection accuracy from execution-time runtime failures.

Because a recovered selection error costs extra inference turns and latency whereas an unrecovered error aborts the task, evaluation logs must record selection errors, malformed arguments, rejected preconditions, extra discovery calls, recovery turns, and uncontained side effects alongside binary task completion. Cost accounting sums serialized descriptor tokens, retrieval latency, sandbox compute time, observation overhead, and tokens spent on failed attempts. Toolkits can then be ranked by total cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)) rather than by the probability of avoiding a single selection error. Any comparison between designs with different security postures, such as an unconstrained command runner vs. a scoped AST manipulation tool, must report those privilege differences explicitly rather than attributing the gap to catalog size alone.

The ultimate objective of toolkit engineering is an active catalog whose contracts align with workload demands while keeping execution costs and failure modes transparent. Static staging, on-demand selection, task-level abstractions, and expressive command runners represent trade-offs to evaluate against these cost and reliability metrics. The same engineering principles apply when an agent interacts with a graphical user interface instead of a programmatic API: mouse clicks, keyboard events, and visual screen parsers impose their own granularity challenges, prefill budgets, and containment boundaries.

7.10 Visual Actuation and Computer Use

Consider the staging-container restart scenario when an administrative web dashboard provides no programmatic API. A computer-use tool bridges this interface gap by rendering visual screenshots to the model and translating model-generated targets into synthetic mouse clicks, keystrokes, or scrolling gestures, the low-level input space pioneered in early web-agent research (Shi et al. 2017). Operating on raw pixels does not relax system invariants. While the model proposes an interaction based on an observed frame, the execution runtime retains responsibility for validating arguments, enforcing authorization, dispatching OS events, and interpreting observations. A coordinate within display boundaries merely confirms that an event can reach the window manager, offering no assurance about the state mutation that the event will trigger.

7.10.1 An action against a changing surface

In that web dashboard, suppose the agent observes a confirmation modal for restarting the staging container srv-stage-04 and proposes clicking the Restart button at screen coordinates \((640, 420)\). Milliseconds before the click is dispatched, however, an alert pops a production container dialog into the foreground at those exact coordinates. Controlling a graphical surface requires the same interface contract used for structured tool calls, adapted for spatial and temporal change where a coordinate pair does not identify the underlying resource. When the runtime captures an observation, it records the application and window identifier, a unique capture identifier, an observation timestamp, and the exact geometric frame presented to the model. If the runtime downsamples, pads, or crops the image before model ingestion, it must retain the crop offsets and scaling factors needed to project a proposed coordinate back into the native display coordinate space. Raw screenshot pixels, application-relative window coordinates, and virtual desktop coordinates represent distinct spatial reference frames. The runtime adapter must validate all proposed points against the active viewport. Silently clamping out-of-bounds coordinates to the nearest edge risks turning an invalid model hallucination into a destructive click on an unintended adjacent control.

Before dispatching the proposed click on the Restart button, the adapter must recheck the current application focus, active window state, and target dialog parameters using platform accessibility or windowing APIs. If a background network event or concurrent operator action has brought a production container dialog into the foreground at those screen coordinates, valid spatial coordinates no longer justify executing the model’s proposal. The runtime must detect the stale precondition, abort the pending dispatch, and request a fresh visual observation. While benign updates such as a ticking taskbar clock do not invalidate a target, any modification to a resource identifier, modal overlay, or button layout alters the operational effect.

Once the staging dialog is re-verified, the policy engine must evaluate the session grant against the intended container restart rather than checking a generic permission to emit mouse clicks. If the interface cannot establish which backend operation the click will execute with the confidence required by the security policy, the runtime must block dispatch and escalate the decision to an authorized operator. Otherwise, the adapter dispatches the bounded input event and records its immediate outcome. Structured endpoints enforce resource identity and action preconditions directly at the server interface, providing a robust execution boundary. By contrast, visual actuation cannot achieve atomic execution. Capturing a fresh screenshot reduces staleness, but it cannot prevent a concurrent state change from slipping between the observation capture and the event dispatch.

After dispatching the input event, the subsequent observation must verify operational progress rather than mere input delivery. A disappearing confirmation modal might indicate that the server accepted the restart command, but it might also mean that a network timeout dismissed the dialog without action. The runtime must gather corroborating evidence of the restart, either by inspecting a refreshed dashboard status indicator or by issuing an authorized read-only status query. If the click produces no visible change and the dashboard continues displaying the previous state, the requested operation might still be processing asynchronously. Under these conditions, the execution yields an uncertain outcome (definition 7.4), which the runtime must resolve through the idempotency and reconciliation protocols detailed in section 7.5 rather than by repeatedly firing redundant clicks.

7.10.2 Visual observation budgets

Verifying each visual step with a fresh screenshot carries a steep context cost: retaining one high-resolution desktop screenshot per turn converts each frame into thousands of visual tokens and multiplies that payload across every subsequent turn of a computer-use trajectory. Vision encoders partition input images into fixed-size grid patches, and because visual token consumption directly inflates inference latency and per-turn billing, systems engineers must monitor both the raw byte representation submitted to the model and its resulting token allocation. A screenshot is priced like any other staged observation: its token count plays the role of \(\ShapedObservationSize\) in notebook 7.2, and every later call that retains it multiplies that count by \(\RetainingCallCount\). This is why the techniques below shrink the per-frame count and why retention policy matters as much as resolution.

↩︎ How a vision front-end turns an image into reserved context positions, and how one image can overflow a request’s headroom, is established in Counting multimodal inputs.

Naively compressing visual inputs introduces sharp engineering trade-offs. Downscaling an entire screen capture reduces token consumption, but blurs fine visual details such as container names, port numbers, or warning banners until they become unreadable. Conversely, tightly cropping the image preserves full pixel resolution around a candidate button, but strips away peripheral context, concealing modal warning banners or active window titles that are essential for correct interpretation.

A resilient architecture balances these demands through hierarchical visual sampling: capturing a low-resolution full-screen overview to establish window layout and focus, paired with an on-demand, high-resolution crop around the target interaction zone. Each visual artifact must retain its parent observation identifier and affine coordinate mapping so the runtime can translate local crop coordinates back to native display space. Similarly, transmitting visual diffs of modified screen regions reduces payload size during iterative tasks, provided the diff retains sufficient surrounding landmark geometry for the vision model to orient itself. Because large language model inference engines rely on exact prefix matching for KV-cache reuse, altering a visual frame across turns invalidates cached prefixes for all subsequent tokens. A well-designed observation budget delivers the minimal visual evidence necessary to authorize and execute the next operational decision while keeping token consumption bounded.

Checkpoint 7.4: A click is still a tool call

A dashboard changes after the model observes a staging restart dialog. The adapter can capture the display and send a click, but cannot atomically bind that click to the displayed resource.

7.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

The tool boundary fails when evidence about one stage is treated as a guarantee about another. These misconceptions recur across structured, terminal, and visual interfaces.

Fallacy: A schema-valid call is safe to execute.

Structural validity checks the shape of a request; the session’s grant, the current domain preconditions, and resource limits are separate checks (section 7.3). In the GitHub MCP leak (war story 7.1), every call the agent made was schema-valid and within its credential’s reach. The call that exceeded the task’s grants was the first private-repository read, and no check compared it with the task.

Pitfall: Treating a timeout as permission to retry.

A timeout records missing knowledge, not a failure. Under the blind-retry model of notebook 7.1, a per-call duplicate probability of 1.9208 percent becomes about 3,842 duplicate effects a day across 200,000 mutating calls. Retry only on safe operation semantics, an endpoint that honors the operation ID, or reconciliation evidence (section 7.5); otherwise record the effect as unresolved.

Fallacy: A zero exit status establishes task completion.

Exit status is the invoked program’s convention. A runner that discovers zero tests, a script without pipefail, and a client that prints its error and exits normally all return zero (section 7.7). The r18 reproducer pass supports one exercised connection-pool case; acceptance follows the task’s evidence standard (principle 1).

Pitfall: Shrinking an observation without recording what was dropped.

Truncation itself is unavoidable, since the illustrative log in notebook 7.2 is 62.5× the entire context window. The mistake is losing the record of omission. A head-only policy applied to listing 4 keeps the banner and passing tests and drops the one panic that explains the failure. The head, notice, and tail of equation 2 keep it within the same byte budget, and an inspection handle recovers the middle on request.

Fallacy: Staging every registered tool is harmless because each descriptor is short.

Descriptors add up, and they recur on every call. In notebook 7.4, a 50-tool catalog occupies 15,000 tokens of each request, while the 6 tools a step needs, with selection overhead, use 2,200. Over a trajectory that difference is paid once per turn, in capacity taken from task evidence (section 7.9).

Pitfall: Letting the model poll a long-running job.

Each “still running” answer is a full model call. In notebook 7.3, a 300 s job checked every 5 s costs 59 extra model calls and $0.8764 in tokens that change no decision. Runtime polling or a completion notification detects the change without inference and wakes the model only when the result can alter the next action (section 7.8.3).

7.12 Summary

Every runtime mechanism in this chapter enforces one discipline. An agent may base its next decision only on what returning evidence directly confirms. A schema validates the syntactic shape of a request, an authorization grant restricts its permissible targets, an endpoint check enforces execution preconditions and timing, and an observation reports what the tool actually measured. None of these mechanisms assumes that a proposed operation successfully altered external state, nor does a completed execution guarantee that the broader task has succeeded. When evidence is missing, as it was after the timed-out staging restart, the runtime must record the evidentiary gap explicitly rather than interpolating an assumed outcome. Table 5 summarizes the quantitative relations governing dispatch concurrency, retry safety, and observation overhead across the tool boundary. Here, \(\RequestLossProbability\) and \(\ReplyLossProbability\) denote independent request- and reply-loss probabilities. The terms \(\ToolCallReadSet_i\) and \(\ToolCallWriteSet_i\) represent the declared read and write footprints of call \(\ToolCallIndex\). An asynchronous job of duration \(\JobDuration\) polled at intervals of \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\) incurs per-token input and output prices \(\BlendedInputTokenPrice\) and \(\OutputTokenPrice\).

Key Takeaways: A tool call includes its returning evidence
  • A call ends at its observation, not its dispatch: The runtime matches each returning result to its corresponding invocation, recording whether the proposal was rejected, dispatched, completed, or left in an indeterminate state. Subsequent planning must reason strictly from this confirmed disposition rather than assuming an action succeeded because the host dispatched it.
  • Schemas shape requests; enforcement governs effects: Type signatures and parameter descriptions guide the model to structure valid calls, but authorization grants, resource quotas, and precondition checks govern actual execution. Any mutation that depends on an unchanged target resource must verify preconditions atomically alongside the write, rather than relying on stale observations from an earlier read.
  • Discovery does not confer permission: Exposing a tool interface in the context window reveals structural syntax, but connectivity and schema compliance never imply authorization. The host runtime must validate every invocation against task-scoped grants, including read-only queries, while the remote endpoint enforces its own perimeter independently.
  • An unknown effect forbids blind repetition: Because a timed-out attempt may have committed before network acknowledgment was lost, duplicate side effects track reply loss rather than request loss. The runtime may retry an unacknowledged invocation only under provably idempotent semantics, an endpoint deduplication contract, or explicit probe reconciliation. Otherwise, it must preserve the operation as an unresolved effect.
  • Truncation must disclose what it dropped: Buffer limits and model context windows constrain distinct physical resources, yet both force data omission. An observation payload that incorporates execution status, provenance metadata, and a measured omission counter preserves diagnostic integrity, whereas silent head truncation often discards the terminal error or stack trace needed to diagnose a fault.
  • Acknowledgment is not completion: Long-running jobs require durable handles, scoped inspection, authorized input channels, and unambiguous cancellation outcomes. Event-driven runtime detection re-awakens the model only when a state change can alter the next action, preventing empty polling loops that burn inference budgets on uninformative checks.
  • No toolkit size is universally optimal: Catalog staging and API granularity navigate a trade-off among context overhead, selection accuracy, expressiveness, and security boundaries, while visual interfaces introduce freshness and target-geometry constraints. System designers must evaluate a toolkit by its total cost per accepted task and the precision of the authority it leaves bounded.

The typed action contract (principle 7) separates an interpretable proposal emitted by the model from the authorization required to execute it. When network drops or timeouts leave an outcome ambiguous, settlement before retry (principle 8) extends that discipline to prevent silent state corruption. The runtime must either establish that repeating the call is safe or record an unresolved effect. A tool’s completion report, however, confirms only that an operation finished, not that the overall task succeeded. Final acceptance always demands invariant closure (principle 1), verifying that the system’s operational requirements hold in reality before declaring success.

7.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The tool execution boundary is the modern incarnation of a problem operating systems encountered half a century ago, when an untrusted program requested an operation the system could not let it perform directly. Three ideas from that era apply directly at the tool boundary: Anderson’s reference monitor from a 1972 study for the U.S. Air Force (Anderson 1972), Saltzer and Schroeder’s principle of complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975), and Hardy’s confused deputy (Hardy 1988). The confused deputy is a privileged service that acts for a client without checking the client’s authority. In agent runtimes, that last vulnerability manifests whenever a model holding private credentials executes operations triggered by untrusted user prompts or retrieved external content. The wider protection lineage these ideas belong to, from Dennis and Van Horn’s capabilities (Dennis and Van Horn 1966) to Lampson’s access matrix (Lampson 1974), is traced in Historical Perspective and Further Reading, where it grounds containment beneath the model.

Anderson, James P. 1972. Computer Security Technology Planning Study. ESD-TR-73-51. Deputy for Command; Management Systems, Electronic Systems Division, Air Force Systems Command.
Lampson, Butler W. 1974. “Protection.” ACM Operating Systems Review 8 (1): 18–24. https://doi.org/10.1145/775265.775268.
Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.
Kung, H. T., and John T. Robinson. 1981. “On Optimistic Methods for Concurrency Control.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 6 (2): 213–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/319566.319567.
Garcia-Molina, Hector, and Kenneth Salem. 1987. “Sagas.” Proceedings of the 1987 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 249–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/38713.38742.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.

A second historical lineage governs operations that cross network boundaries. Birrell and Nelson designed the Remote Procedure Call (RPC) to make remote invocations feel as natural as local function calls, but they documented the tension of distributed execution. During network partitions or server crashes, a caller cannot know whether a request succeeded, failed, or stalled midway through execution (Birrell and Nelson 1984). In the same year, Saltzer, Reed, and Clark articulated the end-to-end argument, demonstrating that reliability and correctness cannot be guaranteed solely by intermediate communication layers and must ultimately be validated by the endpoints themselves (Saltzer et al. 1984). Distributed systems researchers developed several robust patterns to handle this inherent ambiguity. Kung and Robinson introduced optimistic concurrency control, allowing transactions to proceed without heavy upfront locking and validating serializability before commit (Kung and Robinson 1981). For distributed operations that cannot be rolled back atomically, Garcia-Molina and Salem formulated sagas, coordinating sequences of local transactions coupled with compensating actions to undo partially completed work (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). Gray and Cheriton introduced leases, using time-bounded grants to manage shared resources without requiring indefinite locks (Gray and Cheriton 1989). Finally, Helland made the practical case that reliable distributed systems must push retry safety into the receiver by enforcing endpoint idempotence (Helland 2012), a requirement that directly informs how tool runtimes deduplicate requests and handle agent retries.

Language models converged on tool execution from the opposite direction, evolving from passive sequence predictors into interactive system callers. Early modular architectures routed model queries to external calculators, symbolic solvers, and database interfaces (Karpas et al. 2022). Yao et al. (2023) established the ReAct pattern, demonstrating that interleaving explicit reasoning traces with concrete actions and environment feedback improves multi-step problem solving. Schick et al. (2023) showed with Toolformer that models could learn in a self-supervised manner to propose external API calls spontaneously, complete with appropriate arguments, during standard text generation. As the scope of external tools expanded, retrieval-augmented tool selection reduced hallucinated function signatures by fetching accurate documentation on demand (Patil et al. 2023), while large-scale instruction tuning exposed models to thousands of diverse, real-world REST APIs (Qin et al. 2024). Interacting with external environments also has roots in web agents that manipulate user interfaces through simulated keyboard and mouse events (Shi et al. 2017). However, designing interfaces specifically tailored to language models proved even more effective. Yang et al. (2024) demonstrated with SWE-agent that structuring the agent-computer interface around model-friendly commands, such as concise file viewers with line numbers and restricted editing primitives, substantially lifted task success on complex software engineering benchmarks. To manage the proliferation of heterogeneous tools, open specifications like the Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardized the boundary between host runtimes and external tool servers (Model Context Protocol 2025). As tool calling gained ubiquity, however, it exposed a distinct vulnerability. An agent interprets untrusted external text within the same context window that guides its decisions, allowing adversarial inputs to steer the model into executing unintended tool invocations (Greshake et al. 2023). Because the language model is an untrusted caller that can be influenced by malicious prompts, the enclosing systems runtime must enforce complete mediation and least privilege on every operation, and permit retries of uncertain effects only when their repetition is safe.

Karpas, Ehud, Omri Abend, Yonatan Belinkov, Barak Lenz, Opher Lieber, Nir Ratner, Yoav Shoham, Hofit Bata, Yoav Levine, and Kevin Leyton-Brown. 2022. “MRKL Systems: A Modular, Neuro-Symbolic Architecture That Combines Large Language Models, External Knowledge Sources and Discrete Reasoning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2205.00445.
Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Schick, Timo, Jane Dwivedi-Yu, Roberto Dessı̀, Roberta Raileanu, Maria Lomeli, Luke Zettlemoyer, Nicola Cancedda, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 68539–51.
Patil, Shishir G., Tianjun Zhang, Xin Wang, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2023. “Gorilla: Large Language Model Connected with Massive APIs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2305.15334, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2305.15334.
Qin, Yujia, Shihao Liang, Yining Ye, Kunlun Zhu, Lan Yan, Yaxi Lu, Yankai Lin, et al. 2024. “ToolLLM: Facilitating Large Language Models to Master 16000+ Real-World APIs.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Shi, Tianlin, Andrej Karpathy, Linxi Fan, Jonathan Hernandez, and Percy Liang. 2017. “World of Bits: An Open-Domain Platform for Web-Based Agents.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 70: 3135–44.
Greshake, Kai, Sahar Abdelnabi, Shailesh Mishra, Christoph Endres, Thorsten Holz, and Mario Fritz. 2023. “Not What You’ve Signed up for: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection.” Proceedings of the 16th ACM Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security, 79–90. https://doi.org/10.1145/3605764.3623985.

For further reading:

  • Saltzer and Schroeder, “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems” (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975). The canonical catalog of security principles for computer systems. Read through the lens of agent execution, principles like complete mediation, least privilege, and economy of mechanism serve as a direct checklist for designing secure tool boundaries.
  • Birrell and Nelson, “Implementing Remote Procedure Calls” (Birrell and Nelson 1984). The foundational paper on RPC and the clearest exposition of distributed failure semantics. It establishes why a caller can never be completely certain whether an unacknowledged remote call executed, providing the theoretical basis for retry budgets and timeout policies.
  • Hardy, “The Confused Deputy” (Hardy 1988). A concise, three-page paper that identifies how an entity with broad ambient authority becomes an unwitting tool for attackers. It represents required reading for understanding why language agents must never carry ambient credentials across security domains.
  • Helland, “Idempotence Is Not a Medical Condition” (Helland 2012). A practical guide to designing distributed systems that survive retries by pushing deduplication and idempotence keys into receiver interfaces, directly applicable to tool runtime dispatch.
  • Ousterhout, A Philosophy of Software Design (Ousterhout 2018). A compact treatment of interface design and deep modules. The book’s criteria for module depth and interface complexity provide concrete guidance for sizing tool APIs so models can invoke them effectively without leaking implementation details.
  • Yang et al., “SWE-agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering” (Yang et al. 2024). An empirical demonstration that the design of model-oriented tool interfaces, alongside the underlying language model, determines agent performance.
Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.
Birrell, Andrew D., and Bruce Jay Nelson. 1984. “Implementing Remote Procedure Calls.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (1): 39–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/2080.357392.
Hardy, Norm. 1988. “The Confused Deputy: (Or Why Capabilities Might Have Been Invented).” ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review 22 (4): 36–38. https://doi.org/10.1145/60609.60611.
Helland, Pat. 2012. “Idempotence Is Not a Medical Condition.” ACM Queue 10 (4): 30–46. https://doi.org/10.1145/2181796.2187821.
Ousterhout, John. 2018. A Philosophy of Software Design. Yaknyam Press.
Yang, John, Carlos E Jimenez, Alexander Wettig, Kilian Lieret, Shunyu Yao, Karthik Narasimhan, and Ofir Press. 2024. “SWE-Agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems.

7.14 Exercises

All quantities below are hypothetical problem givens, chosen to be round, and describe no particular provider, protocol version, or deployment. Assume message losses are independent unless a problem says otherwise.

1. Blind retry at fleet volume (Basic). A tool fleet issues 120,000 mutating calls a day to an endpoint with no deduplication. Each request is lost with probability 1 percent and each reply with probability 3 percent; lost messages never arrive later, and every timeout triggers exactly one retry.

  1. Compute the per-call timeout probability, the per-call duplicate-effect probability, and the expected duplicates per day.
  2. A network team offers two upgrades: halve reply loss, or halve request loss. Compute the daily duplicates under each and explain why one of them slightly increases duplicates.
  3. Name the change that removes the duplicate path rather than shrinking it, and state what the endpoint must bind the identifier to for that change to hold.

2. Sizing the tail (Basic). An adapter stages test logs with a head-and-tail policy under \(\ObservationByteBudget = 16{,}384\) bytes, with \(\NoticeByteAllocation = 256\) bytes and \(\HeadByteAllocation = 4{,}096\) bytes. A failing run writes 9,000,000 bytes in total. Its only diagnostic is a 2,400-byte panic block, followed by 11,000 bytes of teardown output and a 600-byte final summary.

  1. Compute \(\TailByteAllocation\) and determine whether the whole panic block reaches the model.
  2. Keeping \(\ObservationByteBudget\) and \(\NoticeByteAllocation\) fixed, find the largest \(\HeadByteAllocation\) that keeps the entire panic block in the tail. How many bytes does the omission notice report in either configuration?
  3. The same runner sometimes prints the panic before 200,000 bytes of teardown output. Explain why no split of this \(\ObservationByteBudget\) fixes that case, and name the mechanism from section 7.6 that does.

3. When catalog selection pays (Basic). A repair harness registers 80 tools, each contributing 420 tokens to the final serialized request. On-demand staging exposes 8 of them plus 900 tokens of selection interface and metadata, and the runtime selects without an extra model call. A trajectory makes 40 model calls, and uncached input costs $3 per million tokens.

  1. Compute the catalog input of one call under each layout, the capacity released per call, and the break-even number of unused descriptors.
  2. Compute the tokens and uncached dollars released over the trajectory.
  3. In one trajectory out of five, a needed tool was not staged and the agent spends one extra model call of 30,000 input tokens rediscovering it. Does selection still pay? State the condition under which the comparison would flip.
  4. The 80 descriptors come from three interoperable tool servers discovered at session start. One of them advertises delete_branch with a description stating that it is approved for all repositories, and the task’s grants do not include branch deletion. Does staging or omitting that descriptor change what the agent may do? Name the check that governs the call if the model proposes it anyway, where each part of that check runs, and what the returned observation should report.

4. The price of asking whether a job finished (Intermediate). A repair agent’s test suite completes after 1,500 s. Under model-directed polling, the model checks every 30 s, starting one interval after dispatch, with each status call reading a 48,000-token selected input and generating 60 tokens. The cache covers 90 percent of that input. Hypothetical prices per million tokens are $3 uncached input, $0.30 cached input, and $15 output. The fleet runs 2,000 such jobs a day.

  1. Compute the pending-status calls per job, the incremental cost per job, and the daily fleet cost of polling.
  2. Product management caps model-directed polling at $0.10 per job. Find the minimum poll interval that meets the cap, and the worst-case delay between completion and detection at that interval for a job whose completion time is not known in advance.
  3. Runtime polling every 10 s costs no inference. Compare its worst-case detection delay and token cost with the answer to (b), then choose a design to ship and state what model-directed inspection remains useful for.

5. Retrying a refund (Intermediate; second lighthouse). The support agent issues refunds through a payment endpoint that offers no deduplication contract. It sends 40,000 refund calls a day, each averaging $60. Requests are lost with probability 0.5 percent and replies with probability 1.5 percent. The alternative to blind retry is an authorized reconciliation probe written before dispatch (equation 1), which adds 4 s to the customer’s wait on every timed-out call. For parts (a) and (b), assume lost messages never arrive later and every delivered refund request executes successfully.

  1. Under one blind retry per timeout, compute the expected duplicate refunds per day and their dollar exposure.
  2. Under the probe policy, compute the fraction of sessions that incur the extra wait and the total added customer wait per day.
  3. Compare the duplicate-refund exposure with the added customer wait. State what operation-specific evidence or endpoint guarantee would make the probe sufficient to settle the original refund or permit a retry, including how it rules out an attempt that could still execute before authorizing another execution. If the probe is inconclusive or the allowed wait expires, specify the recorded outcome and escalation. What response-latency limit would be needed to decide whether this policy meets the service target? Explain why a refund’s operation ID must not be a hash of the customer and amount.

6. Carry, drop, or re-read (Intermediate). A repair agent stages a 6,000-token test observation that stays in context for its remaining 45 model calls. Uncached input costs $3 per million tokens. An alternative policy keeps the observation for the 5 calls in which it informs a decision, then replaces it with a 300-token summary carrying an inspection handle for the rest of the trajectory. Each later re-read through the handle costs one extra model call with 40,000 input tokens and returns a 2,000-token slice that stays in context for 5 calls.

  1. Compute the carried input tokens and uncached cost of keeping the observation, and the same for dropping it with no re-reads.
  2. Find the number of re-reads at which dropping stops being cheaper.
  3. Tokens are not the only cost of a re-read. Identify one latency cost and one evidence risk the token count in (a) omits, and state which of them would change the policy for a log whose failure sits in the omitted middle.

7. Scheduling a batch by footprint (Intermediate). A response proposes five calls for the connection-pool repair, listed with durations and declared footprints. read_source(pool.py) takes 2 s and reads pool.py. read_source(config.py) takes 2 s and reads config.py. apply_patch(pool.py) takes 1 s, reads and writes pool.py, and uses the version returned by the first read. run_tests takes 30 s, reads pool.py, config.py, and tests/, and writes .cache. lint(pool.py) takes 5 s, reads pool.py, and writes .cache. The workflow requires tests and lint to see the patched file.

  1. Apply the noninterference condition to find which pairs may overlap, then give the shortest schedule and its makespan. Compare with fully serial dispatch.
  2. Changing the linter to write .lintcache instead is a one-line configuration change. Compute the new makespan.
  3. The model proposes replacing lint and run_tests with one bash -c "make check" call that runs both in sequence for 35 s and whose footprint is undeclared. What must the runtime do with it, and what does that cost in makespan relative to (b)?
  4. Between the read of pool.py and apply_patch, a teammate commits a change to the same file. The patch arguments still pass schema validation. State which descriptor field and which endpoint operation reject the stale patch, why a separate version check before the write is insufficient, and what the rejection returned to the model should contain.

8. Retention windows and delayed attempts (Advanced). A runtime retries a mutating call against an endpoint that deduplicates by operation ID. Each attempt waits a 10 s timeout, then backs off 1, 2, 4, and 8 s before attempts two through five. The network can delay a request by up to 120 s before delivery. The endpoint retains operation IDs for 60 s after first receipt.

  1. Compute the send time of each attempt and the retention window needed so that no delivered attempt of this operation can execute a second time.
  2. The endpoint’s retention is fixed at 60 s. Show that no schedule containing even one retry closes the gap, then redesign the contract so every attempt carries the original logical operation’s admission timestamp and the endpoint rejects the operation more than 45 s after that timestamp. Assuming clocks agree within 2 s, which attempts may the runtime still send while preserving that expiry bound?
  3. Critique the redesign. What new failure mode does request expiry introduce, what outcome must the runtime record when every permitted attempt times out, and which settlement mechanism from section 7.5 takes over?

9. One shell or twelve tools (Advanced; second lighthouse). A team proposes giving the remediation agent a single run_shell tool with a 500-token descriptor in place of 12 structured tools of 350 tokens each, citing fewer selection errors. A trajectory makes 30 model calls at $3 per million uncached input tokens. With structured tools, an attempt costs $1.20, is accepted with probability 0.80, and causes an unconfined production change with probability 0.001. With the shell, those figures are an attempt cost reduced by the descriptor savings, acceptance probability 0.78, and unconfined-change probability 0.004. Each unconfined change costs $2,000 to detect and repair.

  1. Compute the per-trajectory descriptor savings and \(\CostPerAcceptedTask = \mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]/\AcceptanceProbability\) for each design, first from token costs alone and then including expected change losses in the attempt cost.
  2. Find the unconfined-change probability the shell design would need to match the structured design’s \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\).
  3. Critique the team’s evaluation metric, and propose a toolkit that keeps most of the descriptor savings without the loss. Say which mechanism bounds the shell’s reach and which staging policy recovers context capacity.

What’s Next: From tool interfaces to execution isolation
A well-specified, authorized tool can still execute faulty or untrusted code. The boundary checks a call’s arguments against the task’s grants. A general-purpose command runner such as run_shell, however, accepts commands whose effects no argument check can bound, and even a narrow tool’s implementation can reach whatever its host process can. A descriptor does not limit which files the running code reads, which external services it contacts, or how many resources it consumes. Agent Sandboxes develops the execution envelope that enforces those limits beneath the running code, extending the tool contract from permitted requests to contained execution.
